Abstract (英文): Activation steering is a simple, training-free way to modulate LLM behavior, yet existing methods typically inject vectors at a single layer and lack mechanisms for depth-aware propagation or internal verification. We combine two recent ideas - Attention Residuals (AttnRes) providing learnable cross-layer pathways, and Jacobian-lens (J-space) concept readout - into a lightweight depth-aware steering pipeline. On two small open models (Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3.5-0.8B), distributing a persona vector according to AttnRes-style depth weights yields stronger internal alignment (deeper-layer concept cosine and rank, p<0.0001) and stronger behavioral shifts than single-layer injection. The effect is sign-specific: reverse vectors invert all six metrics, random directions degrade alignment (p<0.0001), and a short LoRA fine-tune erases the imprint. Injection rewires attention strictly downstream of the injection point (upstream JS = 0.00000). Three practical findings follow. First, a layer-wise geometric decomposition of cosine readouts: at the injection layer 123% of the increase is arithmetic, while 5-11 layers downstream 100% is dynamical. Second, an intensity window: effects are monotone over 0.3-0.7, then generation collapses (617 to 306 to 25 chars). Third, metric discipline: keyword-rule metrics fail LLM-judge validation (kappa <= 0.09), and two rule-based conclusions were retracted. Training the depth weights on a steering objective (200 steps, frozen backbone) raises final alignment 10x on held-out prompts. Cross-persona vectors retain 96-99% of the effect: specificity holds against random, not semantic, directions. We contribute a practical, low-overhead depth-aware steering method with a careful evaluation protocol. Code and data are released.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Building integrated photovoltaic power forecasting is challenging because facade orientation, rooftop geometry, nearby buildings, vegetation, cloud movement, module temperature, inverter status and edge device reliability jointly affect power generation. This paper proposes an edge-adaptive HieraSeg model for short term BIPV power forecasting with multi-orientation visual-irradiance fusion. RGB facade images, optional thermal cues, irradiance measurements, module temperature, facade orientation, rooftop masks, shading labels, sky condition, historical power and edge device state are aligned into synchronous forecasting windows. HieraSeg extracts hierarchical regions from facade, rooftop, sky, shadow and background images, while the edge-adaptive module changes feature weights under missing frames, latency shifts and local shading changes. The segmentation branch identifies occluded module areas and separates facade, rooftop, sky and background evidence before the forecasting head estimates future BIPV output. Experiments are designed on a simulated multibuilding BIPV dataset with unseen building and unseen orientation tests. The model reports predicted power, confidence interval, dominant evidence, occlusion contribution, orientation contribution and energy-management review priority. This output is intended to show whether the forecast is driven by irradiance change, visible shading, orientation response, stale edge information or recent power history. The framework supports local forecasting at edge controllers with limited communication delay and computing resources, linking visual occlusion, irradiance dynamics and power history to traceable forecasting results for building energy management, microgrid scheduling and BIPV operation planning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The EgoLink Challenge at ACM Multimedia 2026 benchmarks egocentric multimodal intelligence linking first-person perception, social reasoning, and interactive action. Unlike benchmarks centered on visible objects and activities, EgoLink evaluates whether models can interpret emotional and social cues, resolve visually grounded references, and convert user requests into verified tool-mediated actions. Its two tracks cover social reasoning in egocentric video and interactive agents that complete service tasks through dialogue, tool use, and closed-loop execution. Together, they test whether perception, reasoning, and decision-making remain coupled under both offline question answering and state-changing interaction. We highlight a multi-stage Track 1 data pipeline for multiple-choice question (MCQ) generation, chain-of-thought generation, automatic quality grading, and video-contribution filtering. We summarize the task design, evaluation metrics, rules, leaderboards, and representative solutions. The benchmark combines objective answer scoring with process- and result-based evaluation, enabling analysis of both final correctness and execution quality. The best Track 1 system achieved 0.9263 accuracy, while the best Track 2 system achieved 0.844 joint success and 0.916 micro accuracy. Participant reports reveal a shared principle: strong systems make intermediate evidence explicit through candidate verification, option-robust reasoning, scenario-aware planning, tool canonicalization, and state-aware correction before answering or acting. These findings highlight the importance of process verification and evidence-grounded reasoning for reliable egocentric multimodal intelligence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Play is used as an indicator of positive welfare in dairy calves, but openly available video of group-housed calves with individual behaviour annotations is scarce. We describe video from four camera deployments of about one week each in group pens of dairy calves on two farms in the Netherlands: a research and teaching dairy farm (2024 and 2025) and a commercial dairy farm (2024). The recordings comprise 1,173 video files spanning 651 h. For eight observation days, trained observers coded calf behaviour continuously in Noldus The Observer XT with an 18-item ethogram of play and other behaviours, covering 104 h of video, 90 h of which have every calf in the pen coded. The release also contains 1.1 million decoded frames from seven annotated sessions, a per-frame timestamp read by optical character recognition from the camera clock burned into each frame, per-calf segmentation tracks and per-calf frame-level play labels. The data support studies of calf behaviour and welfare and the development and evaluation of automated behaviour recognition.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Interactive video world models serve as simulators for embodied agents: from a starting image and an action trajectory, they generate the views the agent would see. The starting image fixes how high the camera is, for example 0.15 m for a floor robot or 2.2 m for an overhead camera, and a faithful simulator must keep this height as the camera moves. We show that six recent open-weight models fail to do so: as motion replaces the starting view with generated content, they move the camera toward human eye level, a failure we call camera-height regression. Our benchmark, EyeHeight, pairs six rendered elevations (0.15 m to 2.2 m) and four calibrated real-world rig heights with scale-free multi-view height estimation. All six models lose their initial height during a $180^\circ$ turn-and-walk rollout: initialized at 0.15 m, only 0-20% of clips end within $1.3\times$ of the starting height as inferred viewpoints converge to human heights of 1.1 m to 1.5 m, whereas ground-truth renders remain within a few percent. In Matrix-Game models, homography-tracked floor motion stays consistent with constant elevation during rotation even as newly outpainted room geometry yields a higher reconstructed viewpoint. Regression grows with rotation angle, begins while the start frame remains in context, and largely reverses in SANA-WM when a $360^\circ$ turn restores the initial view. We trace this pull to human-centric video priors in which floor-level views are nearly absent: in SANA-WM, textual height cues and dense floor-disparity maps do not prevent it, whereas classifier-free text guidance during the first half of denoising strengthens it. Combining unguided sampling with height-balanced fine-tuning recovers more retention but costs 5 MUSIQ points, a loss fixed during those same early steps. Viewpoint drift alone shifts the paths predicted by visual navigation policies.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Compressing 3D shapes is important for the efficiency of many applications, yet existing methods either cannot leverage dataset-level statistics or use a fixed compression ratio for all shapes. We propose a learned variable-length compression method that exploits structure across a training dataset while adapting compressed representation size to individual shape complexity using only fast feed-forward inference. Our method operates on general latent representations and improves rate-distortion for both meshes and signed distance fields. Code is available at https://github.com/voilalab/varlen-3D.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal models increasingly reason by synthesizing pixels, sketching maze paths within interleaved chains of thought or rolling out video solutions frame by frame. Each visual thought runs the full diffusion schedule inherited from photorealistic synthesis, so iterative denoising dominates inference cost. We study how to allocate test-time compute along two axes: when to render visual thoughts rather than thinking in text or answering directly, and how many denoising steps each thought requires. Controlled interventions that vary only how the thought is rendered uncover three regimes. Causal thoughts carry the plan and become legible early: an 8-step sketch nearly matches a 50-step render in accuracy, commits to the same plan as often as two independent full renders agree, and in video models mostly fails by stalling short of the goal, so the rollout abstains. Decorative thoughts can be omitted without loss, whereas harmful thoughts lower accuracy. Because the regime depends on both model and task, always drawing is suboptimal. Across 14 model-benchmark pairs of three interleaved reasoners, answering directly, without any thinking, wins 9 pairs, and choosing among direct, text-only, and visual tiers from ten labeled examples per pair raises mean accuracy from 60.7% (always drawing) to 65.1%. Where drawing pays, we propose sketch-and-vote, which samples 8-step sketches (a step count chosen without labels) from independent noises until one answer reaches three votes. With this one setting, sketch-and-vote beats full rendering with fewer denoising steps on an image reasoner and two video reasoners, by 8.6 points on VSP spatial planning and by 9.6 and 22.1 points on mazes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Perceptual similarity metrics for natural images transfer poorly to remote sensing (RS), where severe geospatial and semantic failures can remain visually plausible while still being decisively wrong for downstream use. To address this, we develop a novel human-aligned perceptual quality metric for generated RS imagery and evaluate it across several generation tasks. We first conduct a controlled perturbation study where we progressively increase the severity of common synthetic perturbations. Using the layer-wise Aligned Attention Score (AAS) responses in RemoteCLIP, we find that different RS artifacts are best captured at different depths. Based on these findings, we introduce Remote Sensing Perceptual Distance (RSPD), a pairwise metric that averages seven independently calibrated layer distances without tuning on human-preference labels. To evaluate our metric on realistic RS generation tasks, we construct a blind, reference-conditioned two-alternative forced-choice (2AFC) benchmark with five 300-triplet subsets spanning synthetic artifacts, airborne and spaceborne super-resolution, SAR-to-optical translation, and timeseries controls. We also construct a 300-triplet ordered misregistration benchmark with deterministic labels. Using the same RemoteCLIP checkpoint, RSPD substantially outperforms simple cosine distance with the final global embedding and is competitive with the strongest generic baselines across the realistic RS generation tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Deepfake detectors often perform well on the dataset they are trained on, but their performance drops when tested on videos from different datasets. In this paper, we study whether a predictive video model can provide a signal that generalizes better across datasets. Instead of relying only on generator-specific artifacts, as many existing detectors do, we use a model trained to predict natural video and measure the difference between what it predicts and what it observes in a video. Using V-JEPA 2.1, we keep the encoder frozen and adapt the predictor using rank-16 LoRA. We define the difference between the predictor output and the frozen encoder target as the prediction residual. We hypothesize that manipulated clips produce, on average, larger prediction residuals than authentic clips, and use this as our consistency-margin prior. We process these residuals using a lightweight Latent Consistency Head (LCH), which separates residual magnitude from direction for training and classification. To test whether the direction of the consistency margin affects cross-dataset generalization, we compare standard and reversed margin objectives while keeping the remaining training setup fixed. We match the two settings on FF++ validation performance before evaluating them on unseen data. The reversed-margin setting exhibits a larger cross dataset generalization gap on average, providing evidence that the direction of the consistency margin affects cross-dataset transfer. Our complete model adds only 8.25M trainable parameters, which is 2.47% of the total 334M parameters. Trained only on FF++, it achieves an AUC of 90.5 on Celeb-DFv2 and performs well across several other cross-dataset benchmarks. Our ablations show that separating residual magnitude from direction, reducing dataset-specific variation, and using a low-rank projection improve cross-dataset generalization in our experiments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Autonomous driving requires anticipating how traffic will evolve while responding quickly to new observations. Video foundation models offer the first ability, but repeated history encoding and iterative video generation make them costly to use for driving. We present AR-WAM, an efficient streaming world-action model that combines autoregressive temporal modeling with single-pass future conditioning. Because its video backbone is causal, AR-WAM updates its visual context incrementally, reusing past computation and conditioning future prediction on observed motion. We further hypothesize that future features computed from recent observations can improve generalization in dynamic traffic without iterative video generation. AR-WAM therefore obtains these features in one forward pass and uses them to condition trajectory generation, and reinforcement learning then improves the policy through direct optimization of driving rewards. With a single front camera, AR-WAM achieves 91.5 PDMS after supervised training and 94.0 after reinforcement learning on NAVSIM-v1, outperforming the compared strong end-to-end world-action models. In a dynamic setting of continuous driving, supervised AR-WAM keeps its visual history across decisions and outperforms strong world-action baselines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): High-resolution facial datasets with consistent UV-mapped geometry, albedo, and physically based displacement are scarce, yet such assets are essential for photorealistic digital-human rendering. Existing 3D face generation methods are often slow, single-modality, or require large specialized datasets. Meanwhile, large image diffusion models encode strong facial priors but do not natively produce lighting-independent textures, pore-scale displacement, or UV-aligned geometry. We present FaceRiff, a unified, data- and parameter-efficient framework for generating complete 3D face assets (mesh, albedo, and displacement) directly from text in a shared UV space. FaceRiff adapts a pretrained SDXL backbone with task-specific LoRA adapters and uses UV normal maps as geometric conditioning: per-instance normals guide albedo and displacement synthesis, while a dataset-mean normal provides a canonical spatial anchor for mesh generation. Using only 2,049 scans from approximately 350 identities and training on a single GPU, FaceRiff updates less than 3% of the backbone parameters per task and produces render-ready 3D face assets without per-instance optimization. Compared with FaceG2E and DreamFace, FaceRiff produces greater per-prompt diversity while preserving prompt alignment and fixed-topology UV consistency, and additionally generates lighting-independent albedo suitable for PBR relighting and pore-scale displacement absent from prior text-driven baselines. A blind study with 45 industry professionals shows that FaceRiff achieves substantially higher perceived realism than DreamFace, approaching that of real captures. Overall, FaceRiff demonstrates that pretrained diffusion priors can be adapted to unified 3D face asset synthesis under limited data.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Physically based facial assets require 4K albedo and displacement maps for cinematic close-ups, yet generative pipelines commonly produce them at only 1K. Naively applying photographic super-resolution is a poor fit: facial UV maps have domain-specific statistics, and displacement is a scalar height field whose errors directly distort geometry. We present a domain-tuned diffusion upscaler for 4x (1024 to 4096) facial asset super-resolution. Starting from a bilinearly upsampled map, we adapt a frozen SDXL backbone per modality with lightweight LoRA modules, while a Tile ControlNet preserves input structure as the diffusion prior restores pore- and wrinkle-scale detail. On held-out maps, our method produces sharper, more faithful detail than bilinear and off-the-shelf GAN and diffusion super-resolvers, reducing albedo LPIPS from 0.21 to 0.08 while avoiding misplaced or excessive displacement relief. These results show that lightweight domain adaptation can produce render-ready 4K facial asset maps without a custom super-resolution architecture.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Label-free detection of viral infection is central to antiviral screening and pathogen surveillance. Most deep-learning methods analyze individual frames and do not model temporal progression. We evaluate temporal pseudo-RGB encoding, which stacks three time-offset brightfield frames ($t{-}6$ h, $t{-}3$ h, $t$) as the red, green, and blue channels of a single image so that morphological change becomes color and an ImageNet-pretrained convolutional neural network (CNN) can be trained on infection trajectories without architectural modification. In human brain microvascular endothelial cells (HBMVEC) infected with Venezuelan equine encephalitis virus (VEEV) TC-83, a multi-task network predicts infected-versus-mock status and time post-infection in one forward pass. Across three experimental runs under leave-one-row-out cross-validation, with all 36 wells scored out of fold, the encoding reaches 91.6% accuracy against 87.4% for the single-frame model (paired difference $+4.2$ percentage points, 95% CI $[+2.5, +6.1]$; sign-flip $p = 3\times10^{-5}$; well-level inference, conditional on three runs). At the late-phase endpoint (40-48 h), where floating cells and debris can make conventional brightfield images difficult to interpret, the advantage widens to $+23.9$ points (95% CI $[+19.2, +28.9]$). Time post-infection is recovered on the fixed row split with MAE = 1.31 h ($R^2 = 0.975$). Among the temporal models tested the encoding has the highest mean accuracy, at a fraction of the cost of the recurrent and 3D convolutional alternatives, though only its margin over the single-frame model is resolved; the tested ViT-B/16 backbone showed no corresponding gain, so the finding is limited to convolutional backbones and this virus-cell system.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual brain decoding from functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) data has made remarkable progress in recent years, driven by increasingly powerful artificial intelligence (AI) models and the growing availability of large-scale public datasets. However, despite the impressive advances achieved in static image decoding, the reconstruction of videos from fMRI signals remains challenging due to the temporal mismatch between rapidly changing video stimuli and the slow dynamics of the blood-oxygen-level-dependent (BOLD) response. We evaluate a Bayesian-inspired proposal-and-rerank strategy on CC2017 and the BOLD Moments Dataset (BMD), using a linear semantic decoder, a pretrained video generator, and three neural encoding streams with ridge readouts and learned pooling in two streams. We refer to this framework as REEL (Reranking Encoder Embeddings with Linear decoding). Compared with a single generated candidate, reranking increases mean Video 50-way accuracy from 0.2359 to 0.2434 across three CC2017 participants and from 0.3653 to 0.3920 across ten BMD participants. These findings suggest an alternative and conceptually simpler strategy for improving video brain decoding, highlighting the potential of our approach as a complementary direction to increasingly complex pipelines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Published face-privacy methods report protection above 90% against black-box recognition models. That number comes from an evaluation protocol with two independent defects, reported in a measure that cannot size either one. The input is wrong: methods published since 2022 feed the network the whole photograph, or a detector's box, where deployed systems detect, align to a landmark template and crop. Re-scoring the same files through a realistic front end costs a standard transfer attack 56% of its dodging protection and a budget-allocating attack 7%, with no image regenerated. The measure is wrong: that loss is a difference of success rates, so it saturates, moving by 8.2x over a budget sweep while the effect beneath it moves by 1.34x. We therefore report the paired cosine shift, in which both thresholds cancel algebraically, so the input defect holds without committing to a calibration. Measured that way it is 2.2x and 10.7x larger on three unseen recognizers, and at matched budget it reverses the L-infinity transfer ranking: the one surveyed method that aligns at evaluation gains 0.18 where MI-FGSM loses 0.05. The cause is a resampling mismatch, not perturbation lost outside the crop; optimising through a differentiable alignment operator largely removes it, adding +24 to +58 points on unseen recognizers and a commercial system, with a residual on one of them. The thresholds are wrong: the four constants inherited since 2021 hold the false-accept rate near its nominal 1% on aligned crops but admit 2.3-5.4% on whole images, with an opposite-signed bias across dodging and impersonation, the two tasks reported side by side. Differentiable alignment and gallery scoring are not new: the first generation of this literature used both, and we show what dropping them cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Bird's-Eye-View (BEV)-conditioned large language models (LLMs) enable language-interactive driving, but onboard deployment requires efficient perception and continuous planning alongside autoregressive language generation. We present GridDriver, an efficient BEV-conditioned LLM framework combining trajectory prediction, structured driving indicators, natural-language navigation, driving explanations, and scene question answering. Lightweight spatio-temporal BEV perception and spatially structured tokens provide scene context to a shared LLM, while frame-wise scheduling interleaves trajectory prediction with asynchronous language generation. GridDriver achieves competitive performance on public benchmarks, reaching 87.9% PDMS on NAVSIM and 57.6% accuracy on nuScenes-QA with a shared planning and QA checkpoint. Spatial representation analyses demonstrate improved geometric information retention and trajectory prediction compared with Q-Former compression. The system achieves 32.7 ms standalone planning latency and 9.4 plans/s in an onboard ROS 2 pipeline. We further evaluate planning on our own data recordings and demonstrate open-loop onboard operation on a research vehicle. Across public benchmarks and real-world evaluations, we identify strengths and limitations of the BEV-based approach.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A grounded reasoning chain cites a frame span and a box for every step, so a reader can check where the model looked. Whether the answer depends on the cited region is a different question. We test it by intervention: the regions a model cited in its own chain are blacked out, and the share of correct answers that flip is compared with blacking out boxes of the same size elsewhere on the same frames. The paired difference, the cite gap, needs no annotation. We apply it to 2-3B video-language models trained on exact CLEVRER chains and on pseudo-labelled NExT-QA chains. With the same weights, the cite gap is 0.089 on CLEVRER and 0.003 on NExT-QA. Pooled over models, masking the cited boxes flips 2.2 times as many correct answers as the control on CLEVRER and 1.1 times as many on NExT-QA, also among the answers that depend on the video at all, while the boxes pass a grounding check on both. Masking the cited entity on every frame, instead of the cited box, flips 1.9 times as many answers as its control. The NExT-QA chains were supervised to cite two frames of entities that stay visible on most frames, so this outcome is what the supervision predicts; the point is that the usual check on grounded chains cannot see it. A citation can be verifiable and relevant without the answer depending on it, and a test of grounded reasoning should remove what a chain names, not only where it points.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Making automated vehicles accessible requires consideration of vulnerable road users, including wheelchair users. To offer a more tailored experience that supports independence for this group, vehicle design needs to accommodate the wide range of wheelchair configurations. However, wheelchairs are individualized, varying in frame geometry, propulsion, and visual appearance. Moreover, occupants or accessories may obscure visual cues. Existing mobility-aid datasets only label the presence of a wheelchair, rather than describing its specific configuration. To fill this gap, this paper introduces the Wheelchair Configuration Image Dataset (WCID), comprising 467 face-blurred images across 192 capture groups solely for evaluation. Each image is annotated with (i) a two-class label of manual or powered, (ii) a chair subtype, (iii) occupancy, viewpoint, setting, lighting, occlusion, propulsion, and control, with a 273-image validation split and 194-image test split. Three convolutional neural network backbones and a frozen vision-language probe are trained on an external image dataset and evaluated on WCID to measure cross-dataset generalization, while also testing robustness using Gaussian blur. Convolutional models do well on clean images, but performance drops under strong blur, leaving a gap before deployment. Code, split assignments, labels, trained weights, and the face-blurred images is available at: https://github.com/Mi3-Lab/MAID

</details>

Abstract (英文): Worldwide neuroimaging data could strengthen diagnostic foundation models, but privacy rules often prevent centralizing brain MRI scans. We present SCAN, a site-aware collaborative adaptation framework for Neurological Disease Classification that lets new sites adapt a 3D medical vision-language model (VLM) without retraining or sharing patient scans. Each site keeps exact class prototypes for local prediction and can share privacy-protected class summaries: norm-bounded sums and counts perturbed once with calibrated noise. Using 7,641 scans from 67 sites across 12 countries, covering Alzheimer's disease (AD), stroke, epilepsy and controls, we assess local adaptation, cross-site benefits, and effects on existing sites. At seven unseen sites, the frozen VLM's zero-shot AUCs were 0.833-0.945 for AD and stroke. Training-free local prototypes improved epilepsy AUC at three sites from 0.598/0.625/0.508 to 0.684/0.729/0.762 and AD AUC at one site from 0.833 to 0.863, nearly matching a classifier trained at each site (mean AUC 0.757 vs. 0.759). With differential privacy ($ε$ = 4), new sites contributed without materially reducing accuracy at existing sites (AUC change $\leq$0.2%), although gains from sharing were limited, and membership attacks performed near chance. SCAN offers hospitals a worldwide framework to adapt and share brain MRI VLMs without exchanging scans or retraining.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A biometric designer controls the number of features and their temporal persistence. Earlier empirical work linked the equal error rate (EER) to persistence and to the required number of features by straight lines. We derive the law behind these regularities. For $N$ independent standardized Gaussian features with test-retest correlation $ρ$, scored by cosine similarity, Fisher's $z$-transform turns the genuine and impostor scores into two normals, giving $\mathrm{EER}=Φ(-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{N}\,\operatorname{atanh}ρ)$ and, inversely, $N=(2q/\operatorname{atanh}ρ)^2$ with $q=Φ^{-1}(1-\mathrm{EER})$; intercorrelated features enter through an effective count $N_{\mathrm{eff}}$. The central extension lets persistence vary across comparisons with spread $τ$ on Fisher's scale: $\mathrm{EER}=Φ(-m/(\sqrt{τ^2+v}+\sqrt{v}))$ with $v=1/(N_{\mathrm{eff}}-2)$, which for a normal spread has a floor $Φ(-m/τ)$ that no number of features can lower. With no fitted parameter the simple law reproduces published simulations and the EER-ICC line, and predicts the KCC-EER relation of eye-movement embeddings on GazeBase ($R^2=0.88$), though less well on GazeBaseVR ($0.63$). Read from the genuine and impostor scores, the general form matches the EER of eye movements, keystroke dynamics, the ECG and face embeddings over 101 conditions with $R^2=0.98$ and a median error of $0.6$ points, against $0.87$ and $1.9$ points for the simple law. The 61 eye-movement, keystroke and ECG conditions check consistency on the same scores; the 40 face conditions use held-out subjects, with the same accuracy. The product $τ\sqrt{N_{\mathrm{eff}}-2}$ gives a design rule: adding or decorrelating features pays fully while it is well below one and little once it exceeds about three, where only higher or more uniform persistence helps.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hospital brain imaging archives hold a diverse array of pathology at sample sizes beyond prospective studies, particularly for underrepresented populations and diseases. To unlock uncurated clinical data and scale analysis, methods are needed to detect abnormalities across contrasts and resolutions. Synthetic data has provided robust, contrast-agnostic models for a variety of human neuroimaging tasks in healthy anatomy, but pathology detection and other tasks in clinical settings still require real patient data, contrast-specific models, or disease-specific generators. We propose PathoDraw, a generative model of brain MRI with pathology. A conditional distribution of lesions given the healthy anatomy is drawn into the label map before contrast-agnostic rendering, and a single network estimates healthy anatomy with exact signed intensity deviation of any input scan. The contrast-agnostic 3D UNet learns the healthy labels under the lesion and the signed deviation directly and identifies pathology via robust tissue statistics and learned features, despite no training on real pathology. We show zero-shot detection on public stroke, tumor, and white matter hyperintensity datasets as well as archived clinical data, spanning $T_1$w, FLAIR and $T_2$w at various resolutions under a recent unsupervised anomaly detection benchmark protocol. Our single model exceeds or performs comparably to contrast-specific anomaly detectors trained on real scans and keeps healthy anatomy intact. The same generator trains networks for super-resolution and for registration in the presence of pathology. Our model, robust across acquisition and disease, enables the use of uncurated, archived data for clinical analysis. Our code is available at https://github.com/agr78/Pathology-Randomizer.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) achieves impressive zero-shot image classification performance by comparing image embeddings against class-specific text embeddings. While recent few-shot methods leverage labeled training samples to improve upon this zero-shot baseline, they often predominantly rely on image embeddings and underutilize the semantic structure of the text embeddings. In this paper, we introduce DEFTS, a data-efficient, training-free few-shot classification method strictly in the text subspace. We first demonstrate empirically that while raw text embeddings can be suboptimal as direct classifiers, the linear subspace they span remains highly discriminative. DEFTS therefore proposes to reduce the image-text modality gap by projecting estimated image prototypes directly into this subspace. Importantly, by expressing these prototypes as linear combinations of text embeddings, DEFTS reduces a high-dimensional estimation problem to the derivation of just a few coefficients. These coefficients are then computed via a Bayesian posterior mean, using a prior naturally centered on the original text embedding coordinates. Extensive experiments across 12 image classification benchmarks demonstrate that DEFTS significantly improves upon existing training-free methods.

</details>

On the former, the decision head's routing attention produces 0.391 mean per-image pixel AUROC, and hiding the image tokens from it leaves detection unchanged. The multimodal LLM's backbone attention does point at the defect, however it falls below a center prior when we change question. It falls below chance if we remove textual context in the question, partly due to backbone attention leaving the object, while the head's map hardly changes. The backbone's states carry the location when probed with normal images, on the test-split images, however, the decision depends on the defect region. A larger model, Clef, that comes with a post-trained Qwen3.8-27B backbone, gives no useful map in the decision head either. We found that it's possible to recover anomaly location by asking question specifically on an image region, retraining the head or reading the backbone, but none matches dedicated few-shot localizers.

Abstract (英文): Visual place recognition (VPR) in forest environments remains challenging because repetitive vegetation, and changes in illumination, season, and viewpoint can make distinct places appear similar and revisited places appear substantially different. In this paper, we investigate whether temporal, linguistic, structural, and geometric context can improve on an appearance-only descriptor, and what limits each representation when it does not. Using MegaLoc as the appearance-based baseline on the WildCross benchmark, we evaluate score-space and descriptor-space sequence matching, vision-language captions and reranking, scene-graph representations, and depth-derived stem graphs across same-direction revisits, extended routes, and reverse traversals. On a same-direction revisit, both sequence matching strategies reach similar Recall@1 (94.1% vs. 91.5%), but recall at 100% precision rises from 50.9% (single-frame MegaLoc) to 71.6% in score space and falls to 36.5% in descriptor space. The remaining context representations provide no consistent improvement over the appearance-based baseline. Same-place captions show limited place-specific discrimination, reranking is constrained by the initial candidate shortlist, and stem-graph matching remains weak even with LiDAR depth.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Portraits shape first impressions, and lighting is an important factor. Prior work, such as lighting estimation and portrait relighting, edits lighting after capture but does not guide where to place a light before the shot. This leaves an open question: where should a light go to best light a face? Therefore, we propose LightMatch, a preference-grounded inverse lighting framework that disentangles the controllable light from ambient illumination and optimizes its placement. We first learn a Bradley-Terry quality model from pairwise human judgments. A perception head then regresses spherical-harmonic lighting from frozen self-supervised features via cross-attention. Finally, a placement solver isolates the light via spherical-harmonic linearity and optimizes its reachable position. On held-out environments, LightMatch attains 87.8% of the oracle improvement, outperforming the conventional solver that ignores ambient light by a relative 28.4% and the same pipeline with the widely used DPR estimator by a relative 70.5%. It reaches 73.2% on real OLAT faces of unseen identities and, on our real captures, cuts lamp-direction error by 37.7%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The effectiveness of a video diffusion model's inference budget depends on where computation is spent along the denoising trajectory. Across eight equal-budget inference configurations, VBench total scores span 4.80 points, highlighting the importance of explicit allocation design. Our key insight is that guidance evaluations are not equally valuable across diffusion time: retaining the sampling updates while selectively reusing guidance can preserve generation quality with fewer denoiser evaluations. We introduce BATMoS (Budget Aware Temporal Mixture of Strategies), which routes between fresh classifier-free guidance and cached guidance using a single pretrained denoiser. A magnitude-based temporal rule assigns guidance reuse to low-magnitude blocks and reserves fresh guidance for the remaining blocks, with a single integer controlling the computation budget. The resulting schedule requires no additional router training or per-budget threshold search. Evaluated on CogVideoX-2B with 4,720 videos per configuration across all sixteen official VBench dimensions, BATMoS achieves the highest total score among the eight allocation policies and comes within 0.10 points of full classifier-free guidance using 20\% fewer branch evaluations. BATMoS also composes with feature reuse, reducing computation from 80 to 62 effective branch evaluations with a 0.11-point decrease in total score. These findings make temporal guidance allocation a practical mechanism for extracting more generation quality from a fixed inference budget.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Distribution planners need installation-level inventories of rooftop photovoltaic (PV) capacity, but interconnection records may be incomplete or spatially aggregated. Conventional image-based estimators segment PV arrays, convert the mask to physical area, and multiply by an estimated power density, coupling capacity prediction to mask-derived area. We propose Direct Head, a segmentation-guided architecture that retains U-Net segmentation and soft-mask pooling while directly regressing nonnegative capacity from PV-region features and area descriptors. We evaluate three independent runs on 7,590 French rooftop installations under the original split and a department-based holdout. Relative to a parameter-matched area-density neural model, Direct Head reduces mean absolute error by 8.7% and 12.5%, and symmetric mean absolute percentage error by 14.0% and 20.7%, respectively. Comparisons with conventional estimators, controlled architecture variants, auxiliary-loss ablations, and representative cases show that segmentation provides useful spatial evidence without requiring capacity to be determined by an area-density product.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) require visual encoders that extract fine-grained visual features while constructing semantically disentangled and discriminative representations. Conventional methodologies encounter a fundamental trade-off: contrastive learning enhances inter-class discrimination by suppressing intra-class variability, whereas generative reconstruction emphasizes perceptual fidelity but lacks explicit comparative supervision. The naive aggregation of these objectives via composite loss functions often induces optimization conflicts and instability due to gradient interference within the shared latent space. To resolve this, we introduce a unified representation-learning paradigm that reformulates generative reconstruction as a discriminative ranking problem within the generative output manifold. Our approach maintains perceptual reconstruction as a supervisory signal and integrates a ranking-based discriminative constraint by systematically ordering matched and mismatched reconstruction outputs. This formulation embeds discriminative information seamlessly into the generative process, alleviating the risk of loss competition. Specifically, we propose AUC Generative Reconstruction (AUCGR), which optimizes the area under the ROC curve (AUC) by ensuring higher scores for matched pairs than for mismatched pairs, and Partial AUC Generative Reconstruction (pAUCGR), which addresses supervision bias by focusing on mismatched comparisons within reliable feature subspaces. The proposed objectives are designed to be plug-and-play, requiring no architectural modifications to existing generative reconstruction frameworks. Theoretical analysis establishes rigorous connections to AUC/pAUC optimization and provides generalization guarantees. Extensive experiments validate that our method advances both discriminative accuracy and fine-grained perceptual representation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Deformation-based 4D Gaussian splatting (4DGS) enables dynamic scene rendering. However, evaluating deformation networks for all Gaussians at every frame is compute-intensive on edge devices. We find that Gaussian trajectories are often locally predictable, yet prediction reliability varies across Gaussians and time. Thus, we propose CueSplat, a post-training framework that exploits local temporal regularity while keeping the pretrained backbone frozen and retaining its Gaussian set. Specifically, CueSplat predicts Gaussian attributes from recent history and uses a lightweight learned router to select exact deformation updates within a scene-specific budget calibrated offline. Selection accounts for prediction reliability and rendering contribution. Selected indices are reused across nearby frames to reduce routing overhead, while selected Gaussians still receive exact updates each frame. To control accumulated prediction error, we use drift signals to schedule three consecutive full-deformation frames that rebuild the prediction history. Evaluated across four mainstream 4DGS algorithms, CueSplat achieves up to $6.74\times$ and $8.15\times$ speedups on Jetson AGX Orin and Orin NX, respectively, with a negligible quality drop (at most $0.16$ dB in dataset-average PSNR).

</details>

Abstract (英文): Color casts are expected to come from the scene or camera, and a retouching model removes them. We report a case where the model leaves one: an ultra-lightweight retouching network renders human skin greener than the original because it under-restores the skin chroma that a degradation removes and, even on clean inputs, adds a small hue shift of its own. A common paired-data-free recipe, synthetic self-supervision (degrade high-quality images, learn to restore them), silently produces this cast while whole-frame PSNR/SSIM stay essentially unchanged. In a representative 6,825-parameter histogram-conditioned network, the cast appears in 52 of 70 portraits under a ground-truth-anchored, white-balance-free protocol. Both a histogram-conditioned and a CNN-conditioned network develop the cast under synthetic self-supervision, yet both are near-neutral in-domain when trained on MIT-Adobe FiveK expert pairs. Image-level objectives miss it: a global-mean green loss worsens the cast (52/70 to 57/70). With zero added parameters, our per-pixel, skin-masked, asymmetric green-excess loss reduces the cast to 1/70 across stratified skin tones (68.1% to 2.2% over the 70,000-image FFHQ corpus; 64.4% to 0.8% on held-out CelebA-HQ photos). In-domain FiveK PSNR/SSIM are unchanged (+0.13 dB, +0.004), at the cost of a small whole-frame CIEDE2000 increase (8.77 to 9.69) and a magenta over-correction where skin is nearly neutral (skin Delta a* -1.5 to +4.4 and CIEDE2000 5.6 to 6.4 on clean inputs): the loss targets the signed green-magenta axis, not total skin-color fidelity. The same loss suppresses the cast across three ultra-light architectures. A whole-frame green metric correlates with skin-region CIEDE2000 at the method level (Spearman rho = 0.89). The final network exports to a 35 KB TFLite model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Adaptive fusion can repair a static ensemble's errors, but can also overturn correct decisions. RetiDual-Select combines retinal specialist RETFound with the image encoder of the medical vision-language model MedSigLIP, separating candidate fusion, routing safety and predictive review. Calibrated product-of-experts (PoE) fusion is the static reference, a small gate proposes an alternative for each image, a validation-frozen harm guard decides whether that candidate may replace the reference, and a separate score prioritizes review. Static PoE reached the highest or joint-highest clean accuracy on all three datasets. At a clean 2% accepted-error target the review policy accepted 94.3% of Kermany-4 images at 99.89% selective accuracy, and 92.4% of OCT-C8 at 98.65%. Both branches were correct on 95.0% and 91.8% of clean images; only 4.4% and 6.2% had exactly one correct branch. Across 15 prespecified synthetic conditions, adaptive gains were concentrated in severe contrast reduction, where this routing opportunity widened roughly ninefold; percentile rescaling largely removed the effect, linking it to compressed dynamic range. In post-hoc validation-only analyses, gain was higher when the gate followed the more reliable branch, and a frozen guard cut the worst-condition OCT-C8 loss from 1.39 to 0.36 percentage points. Adaptive routing was useful when image quality created a reliability opportunity and the gate followed it; otherwise the system retained Static PoE or deferred for review. The findings are bounded by the synthetic perturbations evaluated and do not establish robustness to natural acquisition or scanner shift.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Segmenting small parts on high-resolution 3D scans, such as nails on hand scans and teeth on intraoral scans, poses three challenges: 1) each part covers a tiny fraction of the scan, 2) its boundary is only a subtle change of height and curvature, and 3) it faces its own direction. Existing methods fall into two groups, and both fall short. The first group processes the whole scan at once and sees each part too coarsely. Point and mesh networks must shrink the scan to fit in memory, so a small part keeps only a few of its points. Multi-view methods render images of the whole object, so each part fills only a few pixels and a tilted part is seen from the side. The second group first finds each part and then segments it up close. But these methods find the parts with knowledge about one kind of part, for example the shape of the dental arch for teeth. Such knowledge does not carry over to other kinds of parts. We find that accuracy is limited by how a part is seen, from which direction and at what scale, not by how many points land on it. We therefore present FrameSeg3D. It first predicts a local frame for every part, its center and the direction it faces, from a low-resolution view of the whole scan. It then segments the part in views around its frame normal at the scanner's resolution, so covering the whole scan no longer costs resolution. Because every part arrives centered, facing the camera, and at a fixed scale, one segmentation network can serve different kinds of parts and learn from few labeled masks. We test FrameSeg3D on two kinds of parts, nails and teeth. It outperforms state-of-the-art methods on hand scans (mean Dice 0.970 on nails) and on the Teeth3DS+ test fold (per-tooth IoU 96.5), with the most accurate tooth boundaries (boundary IoU 70.5).

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-vocabulary object detection (OVD) is essential for detecting objects beyond the categories seen during training. Establishing vision-language associations is crucial for recognizing unseen categories, while integrating complementary visual features across levels supports detection at different scales. However, existing methods often provide insufficient textual guidance for object regions, rely on limited cross-modal alignment strategies, and underuse complementary features across levels, limiting their ability to detect unseen categories and objects of varying scales. To address these limitations, we propose a Text-Guided Modality Correction Network (TMCNet) that incorporates textual guidance into region feature extraction, cross-modal alignment, and multi-scale decoding to strengthen region-category semantic correspondence and promote complementary feature fusion across levels. First, a text-guided modality encoder incorporates textual semantics into queries to guide regional feature aggregation, reducing semantic ambiguity in object representations. Second, a cross-modal alignment module strengthens the correspondence between region representations and category semantics through interactions between visual and textual features. Finally, a progressive text correction decoder combines a top-down pathway with lateral connections to progressively fuse text-guided deep semantic features with shallow visual features at the corresponding scales, preserving both semantic information and spatial detail for objects of different sizes. Experiments on two public datasets, COCO-OVD and LVIS-OVD, demonstrate that TMCNet achieves strong performance in open-vocabulary object detection using simple text queries as guidance. Extending TMCNet to open-vocabulary segmentation also yields performance gains, demonstrating its cross-task applicability and extensibility.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Short-video platforms are now a common way for the public to follow unfolding events. Their rapid, visually persuasive format also makes fake news easier to spread. Unlike text-image posts, short-video news interleaves multiple modalities and changing scenes, making it difficult to identify which frames verify a claim. Existing detectors either pool sampled frames homogeneously or select them by text-frame similarity. Both overlook that aligned frames may repeat a misleading claim, whereas less dominant context may challenge it. We present CEVA (Complementary Evidence Verification Approach), to our knowledge the first short-video fake news detection framework to explicitly organize frames by their evidential roles before multimodal fusion. Its Dual-Branch Complementary Reorganization (DBCR) module separates explicit-alignment frames from candidate complementary-context frames, and cross-modal fusion with bidirectional contrastive regularization operates on the reorganized evidence. On FakeSV and FakeTT, CEVA achieves the best overall results among the compared methods. Under fixed frame budgets and backbones, DBCR outperforms uniform, random, and similarity-based selection and improves two structurally different detectors. Together, these results establish pre-fusion evidence organization as an effective and transferable component of short-video fake news detection.

</details>

Abstract (英文): How much does hand motion tell us about what a first-person camera will see next? EgoHandWAM forecasts three-dimensional wrist and fingertip trajectories and a future visual feature from the current image and recent hand states. We train on 751,065 EgoDex windows using a frozen DINOv2 encoder. On held-out development sessions, the action-only model reduces two-second wrist endpoint error from 60.26 to 56.48~mm relative to a stationary forecast. It still underestimates long reaches. Adding future-feature supervision gives nearly identical trajectory accuracy. With the recorded hand history, the visual head reduces smooth L1 error by 1.32\% relative to copying the current feature. Shuffling histories across sessions erases this gain. In a separate four-task probe, adding recorded future displacements to observed context and future tracking-validity masks reduces visual-feature error by 3.93\%. Masks alone give little improvement. A trajectory predicted from the same observations adds little. On two simulated RoboTwin tasks, substituting aligned human examples for an auxiliary batch of robot examples gives no consistent increase in task success across three training seeds.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In medical imaging, the alignment of multi-modal images plays a critical role in providing comprehensive information for image-guided therapies. Despite its importance, multi-modal image registration poses significant challenges due to the complex and often unknown spatial relationships between different image modalities. To address this, we introduce a novel unsupervised translation-based multi-modal registration method, termed Invertible Neural Network-based Registration (INNReg). INNReg consists of an image-to-image translation network that converts multi-modal images into mono-modal counterparts and a registration network that uses the translated mono-modal images to align the multi-modal images. Specifically, to ensure the preservation of geometric consistency after image translation, we introduce an Invertible Neural Network (INN) that leverages a dynamic depthwise convolution-based local attention mechanism. Additionally, we design a novel barrier loss function based on Normalized Mutual Information to impose constraints on the registration network, which enhances the registration accuracy. The superior performance of INNReg is demonstrated through experiments on two public multi-modal medical image datasets, including MRI T1/T2 and MRI/CT pairs. The code is available at https://github.com/MeggieGuo/INNReg

</details>

Abstract (英文): Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) aims to align augmented views while preserving representation diversity and preventing collapse. Existing non-contrastive methods often address these goals using multiple weighted loss terms. We introduce Centroid Spectral Coupling (CSC), a single-term SSL objective that implicitly couples view alignment, representation diversity, and collapse resistance. CSC constructs centroids from independently normalized augmented-view pairs and applies a ridge-based spectral objective to their uncentered second-moment matrix. Preserving centroid magnitudes encodes view agreement while encouraging balanced representation energy across spectral directions. We establish an exact decomposition into nonnegative alignment and spectral-imbalance components, characterize the geometry of global optima, and derive finite-loss guarantees for alignment and diversity. We also show that complete constant-output collapse is a strict saddle in normalized representation space. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Imagenette show that CSC achieves the highest linear Top-1 accuracy among the evaluated non-contrastive methods on all three benchmarks, and the highest kNN Top-1 accuracy on both CIFAR datasets. A ridge-parameter sensitivity study reveals an empirical alignment--diversity trade-off: increasing $κ$ strengthens view alignment but reduces representation effective rank. These results show that a unified spectral objective yields competitive visual representations without explicit contrastive negatives or separately weighted alignment and diversity penalties.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Egocentric 3D hand pose estimators still miss hands at the image border, misplace occluded hands and jitter. Such failures are usually fixed by hand-written inference refinements and by fine-tuning, two interdependent steps that are tuned separately. We present HandRSI, a recursive self-improvement framework for a hand pose system that consists of a video estimator and its executable inference harness. In each round, coding agents refine the harness, and a change is accepted only if it improves a composite of all ten benchmark metrics on held-out data without significantly regressing any of them. The accepted harness then guides the estimator through selective distillation, which supervises hand presence and temporal consistency on frames without ground truth, while ground truth supervises pose. Since the updated estimator may change which refinements remain useful, the harness is re-validated on it. Starting from the released ACE-Ego-Hand model, five rounds reduce MPJPE-p by 35% on HOT3D and 11% on ARCTIC and roughly halve jitter. On HOT3D, HandRSI's MPJPE-p is 16% lower than that of ACE-Ego-Hand fine-tuned on the same ground truth. Under a unified protocol, it outperforms nine re-run published methods on all ten metrics of both benchmarks. Hypervolume selection on the accuracy-speed front yields Lite, Medium and Heavy systems running at 20.7 to 41.1 FPS. This report gives the full method, every stage of every round and the complete protocol. Code, evaluator, harnesses and weights are public.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A vision-language model (VLM) can judge 3-D relations from the picture or from the ground layout. In photographs the two usually agree. We separate them with CueClash, rendered scenes in which the 2-D cue for each of five relations is right on exactly half the statements. The shortcut gap is accuracy when the cue is right minus accuracy when it is wrong. For Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-5.5 and Gemini 3.6 Flash at low reasoning effort it is +18 to +37 percentage points. It is +7 to +29 without two relations whose cue-wrong items are confounded with borderline cases or occlusion. Given ground coordinates and each relation's definition, they answer 99 to 100% correctly. Pixel measurements with camera depths remove most of the gap for objects fitting through openings. The failure lies in the step from the picture to the layout. The maps that the models draw of the scene from above mostly keep the depth order but, like the picture, shorten depth. GPT-5.5's maps stay close to a copy of the picture. Opus 5.5's and Gemini's maps undo about 0.6 of this shortening. Gemini's then shrink all depths by a further uniform factor. Opus 5.5 follows the picture mainly on fits, and its maps correct depth about as much as human observers of real scenes do. The three models also lose accuracy on CV-Bench photographs where a reliable 2-D cue misleads. In two open models the layout information reaches the language model, but their maps lose much of it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In computational pathology, high-magnification whole-slide image analysis preserves tissue detail but requires encoding many patches, whereas lower magnification reduces computation by covering a higher field of view per patch while sacrificing detail. Existing cross-magnification distillation matches features that often encode detail unreachable by low-magnification input, sometimes requiring slide-level labels that limit scalability. To address those issues, we introduce Sparse Regional Evidence Distillation (SRED), which transfers regional event occurrences that encode reachable high-level interactions between details without slide-level labels. We use a sparse autoencoder (SAE) to learn an event dictionary from 20x teacher features. Logical OR pooling records whether each event is detected in any spatially aligned patch, retaining both localized and shared evidence within the corresponding 5x region. A pretrained 5x encoder is fine-tuned to predict these regional targets without slide-level labels. Visual analysis identifies dictionary components responsive to recognizable histological patterns, supporting target interpretation. Compared with native 5x features, SRED improves average macro-F1 by 1.1 points across seven classification and grading cohorts and average C-index by 1.7 points across five survival cohorts. Runtime matches native 5x, with an average 11.58x speedup over 20x.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Skeleton motion identifies people through body proportions and movement style, and skeleton streams are increasingly passed to third-party analytics that run action classifiers trained on raw data. A prior adversarial anonymizer adds a per-frame residual in the raw coordinate space but sees one frame at a time; motion retargeting needs a target skeleton and complete sequences, which rules out online use. We present MIRAGE, a target-free, causal anonymizer that adds an identity-reducing residual to each incoming frame in the raw coordinate space, computed by attention over past frames. One causal, optionally windowed mask governs every attention in a rotary-position encoder-decoder, and key/value caches let the trained function run frame by frame; on all 56,578 NTU 60 sequences, batched and streaming outputs differ by at most 7.2e-6 m. Reported results use an unbounded window, so per-stream state grows with stream length. We evaluate against a passive adversary that applies raw-trained identity models and an adaptive adversary that trains on the anonymizer's output, treating the adaptive adversary as the headline, on NTU RGB+D 60 over five generator seeds, alongside the per-frame residual anonymizer of Moon et al. trained identically. When consumers retrain on the released data, MIRAGE keeps 83.9% action recognition (raw 89.1%; Moon et al. 88.7%) while a retrained identity classifier reaches 43.4% re-identification (RI) over 40 subjects, against 69.4% for Moon et al. and 75.4% on raw data. The strongest adaptive attacker still reaches 49.3% (chance 2.5%), so MIRAGE reduces identity leakage; it does not anonymize. Against raw-trained (frozen) classifiers, MIRAGE lowers RI below the strongest retargeting baseline (10.36% vs. 22.09%), but frozen action recognition falls to 37.04% (vs. 75.82%), so at full residual scale it is not a drop-in replacement for raw input to existing frozen models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reconstructing animatable 3D human avatars from images is important for applications such as gaming and augmented reality. Feedforward methods make avatar reconstruction accessible by reducing inference time to a few seconds, but recent approaches rely heavily on scaling model capacity, training data, and computation. We ask whether such scaling is necessary. To this end, we introduce ARG-Avatar, an efficient feedforward framework with only 68M trainable parameters that achieves SOTA reconstruction quality. Our key insight is that 3D animation and rendering already provide strong geometric correspondences that existing methods largely attempt to learn from data. In light of this, we design the Foreground Avatar Coordinate RoPE attention (FACRoPE) and Intermediate Token Rendering (ITR) pipeline to guide avatar tokens to attend to their corresponding patches. Experiments across multiple datasets show that ARG-Avatar consistently outperforms prior methods when using two or more input images. Compared with LHM++, ARG-Avatar uses approximately 9$\times$ fewer trainable parameters, 4.8$\times$ fewer training sequences, and 14$\times$ less training compute, while running 6$\times$ faster and achieving a 2.7 dB PSNR improvement on the out-of-distribution NeuMan benchmark.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Underwater imaging is a challenging task due to factors such as scattering, absorption, and turbulence, which degrade image quality and limit visibility. In this paper, we propose a novel approach for enhancing underwater images that leverages the benefits of joint learning for simultaneous image enhancement and depth estimation. We introduce Joint-ID, a transformer-based neural network that can obtain high-perceptual image quality and depth information from raw underwater images. Our approach formulates a multi-modal objective function that addresses invalid depth, lack of sharpness, and image degradation based on color and local texture. We design an end-to-end training pipeline that enables joint restoration and depth estimation in a shared hierarchical feature space. Additionally, we propose a synthetic dataset with various distortions and scene depths for multi-task learning. We evaluate Joint-ID on synthetic and standard datasets, as well as real underwater images with diverse spectra and harsh turbidity, demonstrating its effectiveness for underwater image enhancement and depth estimation. Furthermore, we demonstrate the ability of Joint-ID to perform feature matching and saliency detection for visually guided underwater robots. Our proposed method has the potential to improve the visual perception of underwater environments and benefit applications such as oceanography and underwater robotics. Supplements are available at: https://sites.google.com/view/joint-id/home

</details>

Abstract (英文): All-in-one image restoration aims to recover clean images blindly from inputs with various degradation types and levels using a single model. However, the adaptation policy in most existing methods remains implicitly encoded in feature representations, leaving the restoration decision neither explicitly represented nor independently controllable. Since real-world degradations vary spatially, applying uniform updates across the entire image leads to over-smoothing of clean areas and under-correction of localized degradations. To address these limitations, we present ExplicitIR, which represents the restoration policy as two explicit per-pixel fields: Budget, which determines where and how strongly to update, and Style, which selects between narrow and wide context windows. Specifically, ExplicitIR incorporates a Spatial Policy Estimator (SPE) to predict these fields, explicitly representing the restoration policy and capturing spatially-varying degradations, which are then applied by Policy-Adaptive Decoder Blocks (PADB) through adaptive fusion of narrow- and wide-window responses, enabling independent control and precise local modulation. This design enables spatially-adaptive restoration in a single forward pass, without requiring task labels, prompts, or test-time degradation estimators. Extensive experiments on three-task and five-task benchmarks demonstrate that ExplicitIR achieves performance competitive with the strongest all-in-one baselines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Underwater visual systems often suffer from blurry textures and low color contrast due to the inevitable light propagation. These issues can significantly degrade the perception of stable robotic operations. In this paper, we introduce a novel learning-based sensing system that tackles the multidimensional vision tasks in underwater; concretely, we deal with image enhancement, depth estimation, and uncertainty for 3-D visual systems. Also, we propose a TRIDENT model in a fast and lightweight manner; TRIDENT consists of three parallelized decoders and one backbone structure for efficient feature sharing. In addition, it is designed to be trained to express complex parameterization. In experimental evaluation on several standard datasets, we demonstrate that TRIDENT significantly outperforms other existing methods on image enhancement and depth estimation. Despite performing three tasks, our model has better efficiency than the others for both memory size and inference time. Finally, our joint learning approach demonstrates robustness in feature matching and seamlessly extends from 2-D to 3-D vision tasks. Abstract (英文): Dense collaborative mapping is essential for search and rescue, building inspection, and warehouse automation, where panoramic cameras capture the entire surroundings in a single frame. Unlike pinhole agents that share views only at similar headings, panoramic agents observe overlapping scenes at any 3D orientation, yet searching full rotations also produces mutually consistent false loop closures across distinct symmetric rooms. Moreover, continuous monocular scale drift splits rigidly merged maps at revisited surfaces, while depth noise at short baselines distorts loop translation directions. To address these challenges, we propose CoSphere-SLAM, the first multi-agent Gaussian splatting SLAM framework for panoramic cameras, whose spherical loop closure aligns spherical features over $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ via fast Fourier correlation across arbitrary orientations and rejects symmetric false closures through global $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ consensus. Building on these closures, directional global optimization solves a per-keyframe $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ pose graph with log-scale regularization to correct scale drift while separating optical-flow translation directions from depth-based magnitudes. Finally, omnidirectional Gaussian fusion transforms each Gaussian with its keyframe similarity and renders Gaussians at their maximum ray response to jointly refine poses and maps. Experiments on indoor and cross-session multi-agent panoramic benchmarks demonstrate that CoSphere-SLAM achieves state-of-the-art trajectory accuracy and rendering quality among single-agent and multi-agent methods. Project website: https://cosphere-slam.github.io.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scenes captured with handheld and wearable cameras are still shipped to a CUDA workstation for 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) training. Training on the device that captured them, or on its companion phone or laptop, requires more than translating kernels: GPUs and drivers differ in parallel features, memory limits, and command submission, and these differences decide whether a training schedule executes and returns the correct update. We present Cross3DGS, a cross-vendor, cross-platform Vulkan trainer that keeps one optimizer and chooses its execution schedule per device in two steps: rules on device limits, workload size, and observed driver failures exclude the schedules known not to run correctly, and a fixed preference order selects one of the remaining feasible schedules. The same core completes standard-quality training (30k updates, one million Gaussians) on every configuration we measured, spanning NVIDIA, Intel, Arm, Qualcomm, AMD, and Apple GPUs and including Android devices and an iPhone, whereas the existing portable trainers VkSplat and Brush do not run on some of the mobile GPUs. Averaged over 11 scenes on a workstation, Cross3DGS has higher quality than VkSplat with no significant difference in training time, and Brush reports about three times the training time at comparable quality. The final quality of Cross3DGS is nearly identical across devices. A mobile application turns a handheld capture into a viewable model entirely on the device.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reconstructing an articulated object with its 3D movable parts, joint parameters and unseen articulation states from an uncalibrated monocular video is essential for robot manipulation and interactive digital twins. Existing per-scene optimisation methods require minutes to hours and external depth or camera poses, whereas feed-forward 3D foundation models reconstruct dense point maps in one pass without segmenting movable parts or inferring joints. Even on these monocular point maps, regressing or fitting continuous joints directly fails on casual videos, since cross-frame attention blurs moving part boundaries in deep layers, underestimates motion along the viewing ray and leaves continuous axes ill-conditioned under small opening angles. To address these challenges, we propose PhyVGGT, a single-pass framework on a frozen 3D foundation model that anchors moving-part matching and closed-form joint estimation to the undistorted geometry of the stationary base. First, our Part and Motion Decoder fuses intermediate per-frame tokens with global tokens and references part queries to a static-base query to segment 3D parts, align point maps on the static base and predict learned Bingham priors. Second, our Kinematic Inference Layer fits an orthogonal Manhattan frame to static surface normals, selects the joint axis in closed form under a Bingham posterior combining pairwise motion scatter with the learned priors, and solves a linear system for the hinge pivot to drive unseen states. Trained solely on synthetic data, PhyVGGT achieves state-of-the-art monocular performance across five benchmarks, outperforming FAMOS and monocular ArtiPoint, surpassing posed RGB-D ArtiPoint in median error, and running in 6.8 s per 48-frame video against hours of per-scene optimisation in Articulat3D. Project page: https://phyvggt.github.io.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Camouflaged vehicle detection is challenging because camouflage suppresses discriminative cues across sensing modalities. Existing multimodal methods often require precise pixel-level registration and feature-level fusion, creating a trade-off between accuracy and latency. We propose a spectral-polarization-infrared tri-modal detection framework co-designed with a multi-aperture imaging setup to exploit complementary imaging physics. A consensus-guided decision-level fusion strategy combines cross-modal detections within bounded registration tolerances, reducing dependence on precise alignment while maintaining accuracy and real-time performance. We construct a tri-modal camouflaged vehicle dataset containing infrared, polarization, and spectral data. Experiments show that the proposed method achieves 82.7% mean Average Precision (mAP) with an end-to-end latency of 16 ms, outperforming representative feature-level fusion approaches. On the public MID-1K dataset, the framework generalizes to RGB-Depth-Infrared modalities without architectural modification and improves mAP@0.5 by 5.7% over state-of-the-art methods. These results demonstrate the potential of decision-level consensus fusion and multi-aperture sensing for robust and deployable camouflaged vehicle detection.

Abstract (英文): Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) foundation models provide transferable representations for diverse perception tasks in all-weather Earth observation. However, their development often relies on costly large-scale SAR pretraining, raising the question of whether such extensive domain-specific pretraining is necessary. We find that modern multimodal large language models (MLLMs) already provide visual encoders that can outperform dedicated SAR foundation models as downstream initializations. This motivates a new paradigm: reuse general visual capabilities and devote domain training to brief specialization. We therefore introduce SARVIA, an adaptive post-pretraining method that specializes modern MLLMs using self-supervised visual questions with computable answers. These questions are constructed from unlabeled observations through general image tasks that capture spatial relations and SAR-specific tasks that exploit native measurements and SAR--optical correspondence. A shared answer-prediction objective jointly trains low-rank adapters across visual and language components, with loss-driven task sampling. We then export the adapted visual encoder as a standalone backbone. With 600 adaptation steps and 2 GPU-hours, SARVIA outperforms the compared foundation models on all six SAR detection and segmentation benchmarks. Compared with dedicated SAR pretraining, SARVIA reduces domain-training costs by orders of magnitude while improving visual transfer across architectures and scales. This new paradigm shifts the emphasis from repeated, costly pretraining to a reusable specialization process that can benefit from advances in open MLLMs. Dataset and code: https://github.com/zcablii/SARVIA.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Latent diffusion models rely on a decoder to map denoised latents to pixels, adding complexity and inference cost. We introduce Decoding Without a Decoder (DWD), which lets a finetuned diffusion transformer produce final pixels directly through a linear head. Unlike previous methods, where the denoising output leads to final pixels, DWD separates the two roles: the original denoising head continues to drive sampling, then the new DWD head predicts the pixels at the final step. We extend pretrained latent models with DWD efficiently by finetuning only the last few transformer blocks, training the DWD head with pixel quality losses while anchoring the denoising head to the pretrained model to preserve the sample trajectory. Across latent image and video generators, DWD removes the decoder and reduces inference cost while maintaining or even improving pixel quality. On Wan video generation, it visibly reduces detail distortion and flickering, lowering warping error by 23%. Remarkably, the same separation also improves pixel diffusion models that do not even use a decoder: a final-step DWD head produces substantially better texture detail in text-to-image generation, and improves ImageNet FID from 1.55 to 1.43, a new state of the art among pixel flow models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video understanding with multiple multimodal language models requires a communication interface that can transfer complementary visual observations. Text-based communication is interpretable, but introduces an intermediate generation stage and can omit information relevant to subsequent reasoning. We study Cross-Modal StateKV Injection, a provider--carrier framework that communicates through key--value (KV) states at visual-token positions. Provider models observe complementary video frames; their visual KV states are aligned and projected into the carrier representation space and integrated through gated residual updates. Injection preserves the carrier KV-cache sequence length, while retaining the additional computation required by the providers and the communication module. The reported experiments on MLVU and Video-MME compare carrier-only inference, text-based communication, and StateKV with two provider architectures. Results show modest, task-dependent accuracy gains and lower reported latency than the evaluated text-communication baseline, with some categories remaining unchanged or decreasing. Increasing provider-side frame coverage offers additional gains on selected tasks without extending the carrier cache. These findings motivate further study of visual KV communication under explicitly controlled observation and computation budgets.

</details>
