Abstract (英文): Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models predict dense point maps from multiple images in a single pass. Without explicit geometric constraints, however, their predictions are often inconsistent across views and lack fine-grained precision. Recent methods incorporate geometric priors such as camera pose or depth, but use them merely as auxiliary inputs, process each view independently, or refine the geometry only where the priors are available. We present Refin3R, which refines feed-forward point maps with Structure-from-Motion (SfM) points, propagating SfM geometry over the image grid. In its dual-branch architecture, a 2D branch extends SfM geometry beyond its coverage using geometry-aware backbone features, while a 3D branch reasons over the SfM points of all views, capturing multi-view structure that the image grid alone cannot represent. The two branches exchange information bidirectionally to jointly refine each view, yielding dense and accurate point maps. Across ScanNet++, ETH3D, Tanks\&Temples, and out-of-domain human and surgical scenes, Refin3R consistently outperforms feed-forward and geometry-guided methods in point map accuracy while running faster than the strongest geometry-guided baseline.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Event-based cameras provide high temporal resolution, low power consumption, and a high dynamic range, but progress in event-based object detection remains constrained by the scarcity of annotated training data. To solve this issue, this paper introduces the tool UnrealEvent, a synthetic dataset-generation pipeline implemented in Unreal Engine 5.5. The pipeline combines a V2E-inspired event-frame generator with EasySynth semantic-rendering passes, allowing rendered level sequences to be converted into paired event representations and YOLO-format bounding-box annotations without manual labeling. Events are generated from per-pixel log-luminance differences between consecutive frames, with contrast thresholds sampled independently from a normal distribution to emulate the fabrication-induced threshold mismatch of real sensors. Using this pipeline, we provide a synthetic dataset and evaluate YOLOv8 detectors under synthetic, zero-shot real, and real fine-tuning training on eTraM. The results show strong in-domain synthetic performance but a substantial synthetic-to-real gap, especially for pedestrians. Fine-tuning on a small real subset improves real-domain performance, while the remaining gap highlights the importance of asset diversity and sensor calibration.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Audio-visual question answering on lightweight user devices requires balancing speed, memory usage, privacy, and communication. One approach is to run audio-visual encoders on a server and a lightweight language decoder on the user device, keeping questions local while offloading media encoding. However, transferring and processing the full multimodal representation remains costly. We propose a training-free, query-agnostic token pruning framework that constructs a compact, reusable memory before transmission. We define video spatio-temporal blocks and aligned audio windows as players and estimate their importance through Monte Carlo Shapley valuation. We use an embedding coverage objective that combines query-independent utility with cross-modal redundancy calibration and rewards unimodal diversity through marginal coverage gains. We retain the original token embeddings of high-scoring players under a limited token budget and transfer the resulting memory once for reuse across subsequent local questions. Experiments on Video-MME, Daily-Omni, and Video-Holmes using video-SALMONN 2+ (3B) and video-SALMONN 2 Pro (4B) show that our method achieves the best or tied-best QA accuracy among the compared compression methods across all evaluated settings. On Daily-Omni with video-SALMONN 2+ (3B) at 80\% pruning, our method improves accuracy by 12.86--14.62 percentage points over these methods while achieving the lowest device-side LLM prefill and decoding latency, with up to $1.92\times$ decoding speedup at comparable embedding memory usage.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Maritime search and rescue requires locating people in the water quickly, motivating the use of UAVs to survey the search area. During flight, camera motion and the longer exposures used in low light can blur small targets and hinder person detection. Event cameras record brightness changes as they occur, reducing the motion blur associated with longer exposure times. We investigate their use for maritime person detection by generating synthetic events using the v2e event simulator from public MOBDrone rescue videos and retaining the existing annotations. We compare the recurrent vision transformer model RVT-T with YOLOv8n and RT-DETR-R18 trained on exposure-matched images. On 843 test samples from held-out recordings, RVT-T achieves 15.63% person AP50:95, compared with 6.89% for YOLOv8n and 9.44% for RT-DETR-R18 under 100 ms synthetic exposure. Increasing exposure times from 50 to 200 ms progressively reduces frame-based detection accuracy, widening the gap to the event model. To evaluate the feasibility of on-board processing, we deploy our detector on a Jetson Orin Nano platform. The RVT-T model retains its accuracy and requires approximately 136 ms and 2.02 J per sample, allowing real-time processing on the UAV.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Learned lossless codecs make smaller files than common classical codecs, yet the images we archive and train on still use classical codecs. A main reason is time: a learned codec takes seconds to decode a photo that a classical codec opens in milliseconds, and their faster variants give up most of their gain in size. We argue that much of this time is spent waiting, not computing: the network waits for the values it conditions on, and the arithmetic decoder reads them one at a time. We introduce FMT (Fixed-step Multiscale Tree), a lossless image format whose decoding order and entropy coder are designed together so that neither wait grows with the image. FMT stores an image as a coarse-to-fine tree whose steps hold independent values: a GPU predicts each step at once and decodes it in parallel rANS lanes that carry their states from step to step, so an image of any size decodes in a fixed number of sequential steps. On this tree we build a family of bit-exact codecs, from a rule with no network to large networks, and no codec we ran on the same GPU beats any of them on both size and speed. Without a network, FMT beats NVIDIA's HTJ2K on size and speed; with a large one, it decodes Kodak photos 9 to 61X faster than learned codecs we ran on the same A100, at the same size or smaller.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Event cameras offer high temporal resolution and low power consumption, making them promising for speech-related applications. These include automatic lip reading, audiovisual speech analysis, and human-robot interaction, particularly in challenging environments characterized by low illumination or high dynamic range. Event-based lip reading, speaker recognition, and voice activity detection, however, mostly operate on a cropped region of interest (ROI), by hand or by the dataset, and no event-based method supplies that ROI as a spatial region; existing event-based face analysis gives the mouth as landmarks, which presents a mismatch between application and localization. The present work predicts a dense heatmap over the lip region from the full event "frame". Events are encoded as a polarity-separated voxel grid and processed at every location by a two-pathway continuous-time recurrent network (CT-RNN) with learned, log-space bounded time constants acting on the real elapsed time of each bin: a stateless fast pathway and a stateful slow pathway, utilised for refining and anchoring the prediction. The model reaches 69.5% on lips on four unseen speakers and switches target only 2.1 times per second, against 8.9 and 11.0 for convolutional baselines with
  more parameters. Adding a spatial encoder improves precision but fails on a new face scale. On a real event sensor, trained only on simulated events, the proposed model reaches 58.0% on lips against 43.2% for the CNN, with fewer than half its target switches.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Photographic color grading shapes the tone and color of a photograph to achieve a desired look. Text-driven photographic look transfer lets users specify these adjustments in natural language, with the goal of reproducing the requested look across photographs while preserving their content. However, prior research on text-driven color grading uses different datasets, text instructions, target definitions, and metrics. These differences make it difficult to compare how faithfully the methods reproduce the same look. To compare methods under a shared protocol, we introduce LUTBench. We collect 91 three-dimensional lookup tables (3D LUTs) and define one photographic look per LUT with a specified input conversion. Applying these looks to 5,000 MIT-Adobe FiveK photographs produces 455,000 input-target image pairs with reproducible target images. For each look, we use templates to turn measured tone and color changes on fixed color samples into candidate clauses. We use training image measurements to verify applicable clauses before composing the text description. The evaluation measures target fidelity, tests sensitivity to predefined input conversions, and separates generalization to unseen looks from reproduction of seen looks on new photographs. Across 16 methods, stronger text alignment does not guarantee target fidelity. However, most supervised variants improve fidelity on both seen and unseen looks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Identifying whom a person looks at is central to understanding multi-party conversations, yet gaze geometry alone becomes ambiguous as more people appear. Conversations offer cues that geometry lacks, such as who is speaking and what is said, but no gaze dataset provides them for many multi-party scenes. We introduce SORBET, a benchmark of 3,340 samples from 1,578 clips with synchronized speech, transcripts, and attended person labels, covering more multi-party scenes than existing gaze datasets. Prior gaze models struggle to use these cues, which come in different forms, must be linked to the right person, and can conflict with gaze geometry. We propose Nunchi, a framework that constructs a text prompt from the transcript and the outputs of gaze and speaker models, links every cue to the candidates with shared labels, and lets a vision-language model (VLM) weigh gaze geometry against conversational cues to identify the attended person. Nunchi outperforms the strongest existing method by 4.8 percentage points. On SORBET, conversational cues help whereas additional visual cues do not, and the gains hold across three VLMs, with automatic transcripts, and on two existing datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual foundation models are central to physical intelligence, yet their development remains constrained by fragmented training datasets, inconsistent annotation quality, and limited multimodal supervision. To address these challenges, we introduce GameRealm, a large-scale, richly annotated dataset that grounds visual observations in synchronized, game-native supervision. GameRealm encompasses over 120 million RGB frames and 1,000 hours of gameplay footage across all views. Beyond its unprecedented scale, GameRealm provides rich multimodal supervision, including metric depth, camera parameters, synchronized audio, control inputs, and 3D point trajectories. Its diversity further extends to flexible view configurations, spanning single-view, stereo, multi-view, and panoramic recordings. To enable scalable acquisition across diverse game environments, we develop a unified data collection pipeline that combines human-operated interaction with agentic exploration. Extensive experiments across diverse visual learning tasks demonstrate that GameRealm consistently strengthens visual foundation models in both visual generation and geometric learning. These results establish GameRealm as a versatile and valuable data resource, unlocking new opportunities for advancing visual intelligence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): $π^4$ is a unified foundation model for 4D scene perception. From diverse, uncalibrated RGB observations in the wild---a single image, an unordered collection, or a video sequence---it recovers dense geometry, camera poses, and metric scale, together with 2D/3D point tracks and moving-object masks for multi-image inputs. It couples a geometry branch with a geometry-conditioned motion branch, which predicts image-space correspondences that are lifted into 3D using reconstructed target-view geometry. Across diverse benchmarks, $π^4$ achieves the best overall average rank in geometry evaluation, alongside competitive performance in single- and multiview 2D/3D point tracking and moving-object segmentation. With a shared backbone, the full geometry-and-motion system contains approximately 0.5B parameters---less than half the size of most baselines. It produces joint reconstructions and tracks in one forward pass, including large-scale scenes with thousands of input images despite training with contexts of at most 64 views. We further present the data and training recipe underlying these capabilities, together with empirical studies of key design choices. Code and models are available at https://github.com/yyfz/Pi4.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video question answering under tight frame budgets requires preserving localized motion and event order, yet current video multimodal large language models (MLLMs) reduce comprehension to static multi-image reasoning by discarding unselected frames and encoding keyframes independently: (1) Spatially, whole-frame pooling dilutes small moving targets into static backgrounds; (2) Temporally, dropping intermediate frames or averaging tokens erases inter-frame motion, confusing direction, speed, and order. We propose VIVID (Visual Inter-frame Variations and Individual Dynamics), which moves beyond static snapshots like human vision: keyframes capture core states while inter-frame Patch Diffs preserve neighboring changes within $K_f$ keyframes. Autonomous Delta-Driven Keyframe Planning (ADKP) selects anchors from a $3\text{ fps}$ pool and feeds surrounding patch differences into Dual-Road Change Memory (DRCM), forming Dynamic Tokens at zero extra anchor tokens alongside a camera-stabilized Active Motion Region of Interest (ROI) and chronological Entity State Chains. Adding $0.22\%$ trainable parameters to a frozen $27\text{B}$ backbone, VIVID-Small ($50$ examples, $\sim 1.5$ GPU hours) beats all eleven baselines in overall accuracy across $8,083$ questions and trails only VIVID-Large ($3,600$ examples), which leads on MotionBench, Video-MME, and TempCompass.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in text-to-image generation have substantially improved image quality, particularly through diffusion-based methods that require multiple inference steps. In contrast, Sphere Encoder offers an efficient generative framework capable of producing images with fewer forward steps. However, extending it to free-form text is nontrivial, because its class-conditioned design lacks cross-modal alignment. To address this challenge, we propose TextSphere, an efficient text-to-image generative framework that extends Sphere Encoder with language conditioning and cross-modal alignment. We incorporate pretrained CLIP text embeddings through adaptive layer normalization (AdaLN) to guide image generation while retaining the original spherical sampling mechanism. To bridge the cross-modal representation gap, we introduce a Spherical Semantic Alignment and Uniformity (SSAU) objective that jointly promotes semantic alignment and representation uniformity on the hypersphere. Experiments on CUB-200-2011, MS-COCO-2014, and Flickr30k demonstrate that TextSphere consistently reduces generation FID (gFID) over the text-conditioned baseline while achieving reconstruction FID (rFID) below 1.0. With only 4 and 6 sampling steps, TextSphere achieves lower gFID than the compared diffusion- and GAN-based models trained from scratch on CUB-200-2011 and MS-COCO-2014, respectively. We further observe that improved reconstruction fidelity does not necessarily yield better generation quality. In addition, we use Partial Least Squares Singular Value Decomposition (PLS-SVD) to analyze cross-modal alignment and modality gap, offering insight into the effectiveness of our approach.

Abstract (英文): A working-memory model must remember stimuli and use its memory flexibly to perform different tasks. Here we introduce a model of working memory for images that combines dynamic activity with temporary associations between image features. We train the model on five tasks, including remembering a sample while other images are presented, categorizing distractor images while remembering the sample, and determining whether an image is new or has been presented before. The same trained model performs all tasks, guided by explicit cues about which response is required. We use this flexible memory model to ask how delay, distraction, and image history affect performance. Delays have little effect on sample-memory performance for the delays we tested, while seeing more images leads to more false alarms even when the target image appeared equally recently in all cases. On the sample-memory-plus-categorization task, the probability of correct responses on all questions decreases as more questions are asked, despite robust sample-memory performance. Finally, the model maintains stable performance across 40 brief trials of each task under the tested conditions. Together, these results demonstrate how a single working-memory system can be flexibly used to perform different tasks, and how its errors depend on both previous images and task demands.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Abstracting structured buildings from limited observations is essential for 3D urban modeling and digital twins. Partial point clouds and overhead images of the same building provide incomplete 3D surface measurements and visual cues to its footprint, roof geometry, and rooftop structures, respectively. This motivates a unified framework that aligns geometric and visual cues in 3D and reconstructs buildings from either or both modalities. Building on this connection, we present Any2Building, a unified model for Level of Detail 2 (LoD2) building reconstruction from partial point clouds, overhead images, or both, with optional coarse geometric priors. At its core is an occupancy-grounded shared spatial queries representation that organizes available observations within a common 3D grid. Supervised by complete building surface voxels, these queries connect observational evidence to target geometry, enabling a shared generator to learn structural priors across tasks and providing spatial guidance for recovering missing structure. A trained voxel conditional surface-generation and low-poly remeshing pipeline then converts the predicted structure into compact LoD2 building models. To support this framework, we introduce BuildingWorld-Image, comprising approximately one million paired samples of partial point clouds, overhead images, and LoD2 building models across 18 cities. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that Any2Building outperforms existing methods in geometric reconstruction accuracy across unimodal and multimodal point-cloud completion, single-image building generation, and prior-assisted generation, while maintaining detailed and robust recovery when point cloud input modality is degraded.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Human visual reasoning is driven by systematic exploration and extraction of task-relevant visual evidence. Human gaze provides a window into this process, making gaze a useful source of guidance for visually-grounded reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs). However, human gaze also includes noise and redundancies that may transfer poorly to VLMs. In this work, we investigate how human gaze data can be effectively leveraged to improve visual reasoning in VLMs, while maintaining alignment with human behavior. We collect 7,109 human gaze trials from a diverse and challenging set of visual reasoning tasks, and train grounded reasoning models with different forms of gaze guidance. Across evaluations in chart understanding, visual search, and counting, we find that using human gaze as a prior for grounded reasoning consistently outperforms supervision using the full gaze sequence. Based on these findings, we introduce GGR (Gaze-Guided Reasoner), which achieves greater human alignment and more reliable visual grounding while matching or outperforming leading grounded-reasoning baselines in accuracy. Reinforcement learning further improves task accuracy, gaze alignment, and grounding quality despite using no gaze supervision. Behavioral analyses show that gaze supervised models exhibit more visual exploration before arriving at an answer. Our results suggest that the value of human gaze lies in guiding spatial exploration and shaping reasoning behavior without requiring complete imitation of human behavior.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Human cognitive state (e.g., distraction, stress), gaze, and behavior interact with the driving environment in subtle ways that AI could use to assist drivers, but ground-truth labels for human cognitive state levels are costly to obtain at scale. We propose a multimodal masked-diffusion autoencoder, ID-MAE, that adds a self-supervised objective to impairment prediction: alongside predicting the impairment label as a masked token, it reconstructs masked gaze and trajectory. To probe the effects of different modalities and types of impairments on model inference, we introduce DIVERT, a driving-simulator dataset containing 103 hours of driving data (n = 109) with ten impairment inductions spanning cognitive, visual, and emotional states, recorded with gaze, vehicle state, and scene video. We demonstrate how ID-MAE affords significant improvements in impairment inference results on both the DIVERT dataset and existing datasets, and enables strong results in additional tasks, such as trajectory and gaze prediction. For location-matched pairs, our model achieves a paired AUC of 0.706 for impairment detection on DIVERT, compared to 0.590 without reconstruction loss, improving over existing baselines. We probe the design space in terms of input modalities, masking scheme, positional encoding, attention mechanism, and decoding. We also investigate the interaction between various impairments in training and inference, demonstrating that visually demanding distractions are separable from cognitive distractions. Code and data will be available upon acceptance.

Abstract (英文): Histopathological analysis of whole-slide images (WSIs) plays a critical role in cancer diagnosis, and multiple instance learning (MIL) is widely adopted for WSI analysis under weak supervision. As new tumor subtypes are introduced, extending existing MIL models requires learning new classes while retaining knowledge of previously learned ones. This presents a class-incremental learning challenge, where models must acquire new classes with limited access to historical training data and recognize all learned classes without task identifiers at inference. A major obstacle is forgetting, as updates to feature aggregation and prediction modules can degrade performance on previously learned classes. To address this challenge, we propose \textbf{EviSlide-MIL}, a class-incremental WSI framework that integrates multiple learnable queries per class with a shared prediction module. The queries aggregate frozen patch features into class-specific representations, while the shared prediction module limits parameter growth across incremental stages. By freezing queries associated with previously learned classes, EviSlide-MIL preserves their attention patterns for identical inputs, while experience replay and knowledge distillation further mitigate forgetting as shared parameters are updated. Experiments on eight tumor subtypes from The Cancer Genome Atlas demonstrate that EviSlide-MIL achieves improved performance and reduced forgetting compared with competing methods across most learning settings, demonstrating its potential for scalable and knowledge-preserving WSI classification. Code and pretrained models will be released.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Autoregressive video diffusion models generate frames based on new observations and inputs, making them a promising medium for interactive visual experiences. However, existing models are orders of magnitude too slow for fluid interaction. This paper introduces FastFrames, a family of fast autoregressive video diffusion models that achieves interactive latency without compromising quality. We profile the inference pipeline of state-of-the-art models and find that operations often treated as fixed implementation details, such as VAE decoding and KV cache updates, in fact account for over half of end-to-end inference latency. We challenge the assumption that these components are indispensable and replace them with lightweight alternatives. Our 1.3B variant runs at 36 ms per frame, or 28 responses per second (RPS), on a single B200 GPU while matching the generation quality of a state-of-the-art model that is nearly 8x slower. Our 14B variant runs at 45 ms per frame, or 22 RPS, on an 8x B200 GPU node and yields significantly better visual quality and realism. We show that FastFrames enables real-time interaction in applications such as camera-conditioned and physics-conditioned video generation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Applications such as visual accessibility agents and robotics require answering questions about recent video content promptly, over a bounded window of past frames (e.g., the last two minutes). Hybrid vision-language models (VLMs), which interleave attention with linear recurrent layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), are attractive for this setting, but maintaining a sliding window is expensive. The reason is that recurrent layers combine all past frames into a shared state, so expiring old frames normally requires re-running the entire window through the model. We observe that the recurrent update is affine in the state with input-dependent coefficients, so the effect of each frame can be captured as a transformation that composes associatively. We present Spool, a training-free framework that caches per-frame state transformations and composes them efficiently as the window advances, removing expired frames from each recurrent layer's state without replaying frames through the backbone. Removal is exact within each layer; as with key-value eviction in attention, expired frames can retain residual influence through the cached inputs computed by earlier layers. We design a dedicated data structure and GPU kernels to achieve low caching and composition overhead. Across five open-source hybrid VLMs and four video reasoning benchmarks, Spool achieves the lowest p95 time-to-first-token in all 20 model-benchmark tests, reducing it by a geometric mean of $6.6\times$ over the faster of two recent vLLM baselines, while improving accuracy by 2.3 percentage points on average.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored for knowledge-intensive and safety-critical applications in nuclear engineering. However, existing nuclear-domain benchmarks provide limited content coverage or focus primarily on answer accuracy without systematically evaluating the quality of the underlying reasoning. This study introduces NEBench R1, a reasoning-oriented benchmark comprising 7,546 unique multiple-choice questions extracted from U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) reactor operator licensing examinations covering 18 nuclear facilities. Each question is classified according to the NRC Knowledge and Abilities catalogs and accompanied by an official explanation serving as a reference for reasoning evaluation. Six LLMs were evaluated, with overall accuracy ranging from 34.0% to 73.9% across three distinct performance tiers. To assess reasoning quality, an LLM-based judge evaluated correctly answered responses using a multidimensional rubric. Although responses generally demonstrated strong problem comprehension, they scored lower in factual and procedural accuracy and distractor analysis, indicating that correct answers were not necessarily supported by technically sound justifications. Response clarity was also weakly indicative of technical quality. Furthermore, longer reasoning traces were associated with lower accuracy in both frontier reasoning models, suggesting that reasoning length may reflect question difficulty rather than improved problem-solving performance. Reasoning-token consumption may therefore provide an additional signal for identifying potentially unreliable responses. NEBench R1 establishes a benchmark for evaluating both answer accuracy and reasoning quality, providing a foundation for assessing the domain competence and reliability of LLMs in nuclear engineering.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising tool for detecting hidden goals, off-task behavior, and other failures in AI agents. One risk is that models could deliberately shape their reasoning to make it opaque to monitors. We stress-test this risk through persona-control training: supervised fine-tuning on mathematical reasoning traces rewritten in the voices of diverse personas. This induces a broader ability to control CoT, even though training contains no monitor-evasion examples. In covert side-task evaluations, traces in which a gpt-oss-20b model organism completes the hidden side task contain less monitorable evidence than the base model's. Averaged across evaluations and CoT lengths, weighted by side-task-success counts, the CoT monitorability metric g-mean$^2$ falls from 0.94 for the base model to 0.68 for the model organism without a hiding instruction, and to 0.22 when explicitly instructed to hide. In intervention evaluations, explicit hiding often drives monitorability near zero. The effect extends to a Kimi-K2.6 model organism in agentic environments, though hidden objectives requiring extended reasoning or longer trajectories remain more monitorable than simple single-step side tasks. We further discover that targeted transparency prompts can substantially recover monitorability, sometimes surpassing the base model. These model organisms provide a testbed for studying monitorability failures and potential mitigations. Our code can be found on https://github.com/yongzx/cot-control-model-organism.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Deep networks score near 99% on the MSTAR vehicle benchmark, and the newer ATRNet-STAR benchmark was built to be harder. We ask how much of these scores comes from the vehicle itself. With a modern network (ConvNeXt-Small, 3 seeds) we first reproduce the known MSTAR result: with the central 96x96 pixels zeroed out, accuracy is still 73.3% (chance 10%). The same shortcut is present in ATRNet-STAR, which was collected across several scenes: with the vehicle removed, accuracy is 63.3% on the 40-class SOC split (chance 2.5%). Under the official scene-change split, the background stops helping and starts hurting. Accuracy is 22.8% on full chips and 30.8% when only the vehicle is shown. A SAR foundation model, SARATR-X, leans on the background more than ConvNeXt does (+4 to +6 points with the vehicle removed, in every seed) and does not solve the scene change. We then hold the amount of training data fixed and vary the depression angles used for training. Accuracy is set mainly by the gap to the nearest training angle: 57% at 15 degrees, 39% at 30 degrees and 26% at 45 degrees. Adding more training angles does not help. Finally, when most classes have training images at the test angle and a few do not, the missing classes do worse than when no class has them: 9.0% instead of 22.4% at a 45-degree gap. Most of their images are assigned to known classes. We recommend reporting background-only and target-only controls, and the angle gap, next to any accuracy figure. Code is public.

</details>

Abstract (英文): As AI systems are increasingly used to automate AI R&D, misaligned systems could sabotage research intended to improve model safety and alignment. To stress-test defenses against such sabotage, we use a human expert as a proxy saboteur on AI safety research. We conduct a red-team/blue-team game where a human researcher runs AI safety experiments from scratch and auditors try to tell which were sabotaged and how. Given access to the full research transcript and the ability to run experiments, human auditors mostly fail to distinguish sabotaged from benign research: they achieve an AUROC of 0.59, not significantly better than chance, and fully identify the specific sabotage in only 17% of cases. Despite not being allowed to run experiments, the best frontier models outperform human auditors (Fable 5.1: AUROC 0.73, 32% full identification) within noise and weaker models perform comparably. When the saboteur chooses which experiments are attacked, rather than being required to sabotage every question given, sabotage identification drops further. Our results suggest that research sabotage by expert-level agents is difficult to detect with current auditing methods and provide a new setting for studying research sabotage.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Language models are trained once, frozen and deployed: improving one again takes another training run, and a prompt, a scaffold or extra computation at inference is gone once the task ends. A frontier model has been shown to edit the weights of a much smaller one. We ask whether a model can rewrite its own weights to make itself better, and find that it can, by its own reasoning, with no gradients and no training; we call this self-rewriting. Given its weights, graded answers and the ability to run itself, a 27B model diagnoses its failure and edits the weights behind it. It surpasses itself: its own edit makes it 6.6 points more accurate on 490 problems it never saw, in a test fixed in advance, and another session's edit also makes it significantly better. It keeps getting better: it submits an edit only when its own tests support it, each edit replacing the last, and over its first eight such sessions its accuracy only rises. Unlike test-time scaling, the gain persists in its weights and adds no cost per answer; unlike training, it needs no pre- or post-training infrastructure, only an open model and hours on one GPU. It repairs a damaged copy of itself: it locates the corrupted matrix and rewrites it with values of its own, never reading the undamaged weights; found on text math problems alone, the repair lifts the copy on six image benchmarks it never saw from 7% to 70% accuracy, 83% of the undamaged model's. And the ability emerges with scale: across four sizes of one family, the 2B model makes no edit and the 4B and 9B models break themselves; only the 27B model works on itself as a human would, comparing its hidden states on answers that finish and on answers that run out of tokens, weakening the direction that separates them. Like a human, a model need not separate training from inference; improving a language model may come to take a conversation rather than a training run.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Speculative-diffusion decoding uses a small parallel drafter model to generate b draft tokens in one forward pass, which then undergo parallel verification via a larger target model. Faster inference can be achieved when multiple draft tokens pass verification simultaneously. Recent parallel draft architectures (DFlash, DSpark) improve draft quality by conditioning on rich features computed by the target, through a mechanism known as feature encoding. However, standard feature encoding mechanisms have three limitations: (1) they are lossy, applying encoding projections that are destructive to the underlying target feature-space via a discovered effect known as residual mixing, (2) they are memory-less, with no record of previously processed proposals, and (3) they utilize features sampled uniformly from across the target layers, omitting important hidden states. To address these issues, we propose Mixture-of-Features Attention (MoFA), a lightweight feature-encoding approach equipped with a working memory that encodes previously rejected proposals. Leveraging MoFA, rejection memory, and a set of further enhancements, including 2-tap causal convolutions, prefix-only cross-attention, and non-uniform target layer selection, the final architecture, DNeo, achieves 1.3x faster generation speed than SoTA (DFlash-2) without impacting drafter footprint or generation quality. DNeo marks a new state of the art in high quality, fast LLM inference.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modern sensing systems generate large volumes of heterogeneous data streams from multiple sensor modalities, increasing reliance on datacenters for computation, artificial intelligence/superintelligence (AI/SI) inference, and AI/SI training. Each sensor stream may have different network and compute requirements depending on its type and data volume. However, current wireless systems typically allocate resources at the device level, leading to contention among co-located sensor streams, where bulk data transfers may starve latency-critical telemetry. To this end, this paper introduces Sensor Slicing (SL). SL provides deterministic, medium access control (MAC)-level isolation for individual sensors within a single user equipment (UE) in open radio access networks (O-RAN). Using agentic coding techniques, SL is implemented in two O-RAN-compliant packages, reducing development time by 400 times (2 h 40 min [OCUDU] vs. 6.5 weeks [OAI]), 228-fold fewer human prompts (4 vs. 913), and less than half the tokens (0.64B vs. 1.43B). An associated end-to-end framework, SLED, is implemented to establish per-sensor radio and network isolation across edge, campus, and remote high-performance computing (HPC) tiers. Over-the-air wireless experiments demonstrate that while conventional network slicing causes critical delays to increase more than 2,300-fold under high traffic load, SLED maintains stable latency. Scaling evaluations on the two RAN stacks show that throughput remains within 0.9% and 1.4% of expected allocations even as the number of sensor slices increases. The results demonstrate that SLED preserves latency-critical telemetry while effectively utilizing resources across the sensor-to-HPC pipeline. The SLED software and evaluation datasets are publicly available at https://github.com/UNL-CPN-Lab/SLED-Sensor-SLicing-from-Edge-to-Datacenter.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A reasoning model that has reached a correct answer can lose it by continuing to think. Deciding whether to stop means comparing two probabilities at the same point of a chain: that the answer the model would give if stopped now is correct (its commitment), and that the answer it will finish with is correct (its verdict). We show that the verdict alone, which is what final-correctness probes estimate, cannot make this comparison. As a forecast of a fixed outcome it has no predictable drift, whether continuing helps or hurts. The commitment-verdict gap equals the expected remaining change in answer quality and the accuracy gained by stopping. A second identity splits any accuracy-versus-budget curve into answer change within chains and change in which chains are still running. We measure both on MATH-500. At all 379 points where DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B holds a correct answer, selected without regard to how its chain ends, continuing loses 3.6 points on average (95% CI [2.2, 5.2]), and the loss concentrates in a few problems. In six reasoning models from three families, on MATH-500 and OlympiadBench and with budgets up to 16,384 tokens, declining accuracy curves come from which chains remain, while the chains still reasoning keep or improve their answers. An oracle stopping rule gains at most +1.8 and +2.5 points at 1.5B and 7B. The framework says what to measure before building a stopping rule.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The regulation of Artificial Intelligence is susceptible to various forms of capture by industry actors that hold undue influence over the policymaking process, due in part to their information advantage in developing these technologies. In the following paper, we investigate the roles of public commenters in providing evidence to potential regulation in the USA. We build a corpus of 40 potential regulations, and 2,664 public comments submitted by a variety of stakeholders. We computationally process these texts to extract evidence meant to inform regulators, in the form of formal citations and informal references. We find significant differences in the type and amount of evidence supplied: Holding comment length constant, business commenters introduce altogether less evidence, and government, university, and civil society commenters provide significantly more research and legal sources. The publication status of cited research is nearly evenly split, with preprints and peer-reviewed articles being introduced by commenters of all types. We further explore patterns of self-citation and find that while it is prevalent amongst all groups, a small cluster of organizations account for over half of all self-citations in our corpus. Our findings complicate a narrative that public comments alone are means to information capture. We interpret these results in terms of the role that commenters attempt to play in the policymaking process-being either an Issue Advocate or an Honest Broker. These findings on the supply of evidence in public comments shed light on an often overlooked form of policymaking that has implications for how and through what sources information is made accessible to regulators of AI.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents can assist scientific research, but balancing autonomous investigation with scientists' ability to inspect and redirect ongoing work remains a central design challenge. We present DiscoveryAgent, a domain-agnostic, scientist-centric system that combines autonomous investigation with scientist-directed hypothesis steering. Scientists select, refine, or replace hypotheses between research rounds, while audit agents interrogate checkpointed records to trace claims, reproduce analyses, and inform subsequent decisions. Across two predictive modeling tasks, DiscoveryAgent produced the best variable-star classifier among evaluated baselines and, with scientist steering, a protein variant-effect predictor surpassed by only one of 106 ProteinGym baselines. We develop a rigorous checkpointing system that enables highly capable audit agents, a scalable form of oversight for agentic research. Audit agents autonomously identify errors at higher rates than previous AI scientist agents, and scientists collaborating with audit agents identify 14x more valid errors versus scientists performing manual review. In three case studies led by domain experts across physical science, social science, and life science, we developed a multimodal photometric-redshift estimator that resolves complementary failures of images and photometry, achieving 0.971 AUROC for low-redshift classification; documented an 83% rise in Florida homeowners-insurance premiums from 2013 to 2023, with a growing, regressive burden; and identified CD4+ T-cell relay genes, which both receive and exert broad transcriptional regulation, enriched for autoimmune disease associations. Together, these results show how autonomous research can expand scientists' empirical reach while centering their authority over research direction and their ability to understand and challenge the evidence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models are moving from isolated assistants toward autonomous agents that can plan, use tools, and coordinate with others, motivating an emerging Agentic Web of independently developed agents and services. Realizing this vision requires agent behavior to move across creators, hosts, and runtimes without coupling publication to a particular execution environment or transferring authority over user resources. We introduce WebAgents, which unbundles these roles through a signed agent.pkg that carries portable workflows, skills, schemas, and resource requirements. An OpenClaw-based agent-native browser/runtime verifies the package, obtains authorization, and binds it at run time to user-controlled models, tools, permissions, and budgets, while hosts distribute the public program and may retain private services server-side. We demonstrate this contract with three unrelated workflows executed unchanged under local and API-backed models, including literature review with live services and a website-published travel agent using semantic tools and user-selected personal resources. In a controlled suite, WebAgents explicitly blocks all 716 injected contract violations while accepting all 295 matched benign controls, with median automated pre-execution processing below 0.5 s for all three packages. We conclude by outlining a path toward NANDA-based discovery and interoperable package ecosystems while retaining execution authority in the user's runtime.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Personal assistants accumulate months of conversations, but today's long-term memory stacks put derivation or selection between a question and its evidence. In a system-level comparison on LongMemEval, two commercial assistants scored well below the same model reading the history offline [1], consistent with, but not isolating, content loss between write and read. We propose KV-LTM: a memory tier storing each session's raw text plus its KV cache under a fixed reader model, answering by restoring cached sessions and appending the question. KV-LTM is complementary to, not a replacement for, retrieval and indexing. Text is the memory of record; the cache is a recompute-free prefill accelerator, degrading gracefully to text. We specify the design: storage record, budget-derived retention, append-only read path, and bounded aggregation. We size it analytically: across pinned BF16 full-attention (GQA) and latent-cached MLA configurations, per-token state spans approximately 31K-328K bytes (the pinned KDA+MLA hybrid differs: approximately 8.1K bytes/token plus a fixed per-session recurrent term), giving approximately 0.34-3.6 TB per user-year at a declared 30K-token/day scenario, against approximately 49 MB of raw text; per-query cost is modelled as regime-conditional transfer, attention, decode, aggregation, and compute. We define an evaluation protocol needing no persistent-cache implementation for logical QA evaluation: per-session prefill emulates valid cache reuse under the same model/input-state contract and ideal arithmetic, with named arms, controlled baselines, and a preregistered reader-model gate; a separate minimal real-cache replay tests numerical equivalence and measures the cost axis, with outcomes not assumed. KV-LTM trades storage for avoided re-prefill compute and write-time fidelity, within a declared retention budget.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Counterfactual explanations are often seen as counterfactual conditionals under closest-world semantics, requiring one to reason over all minimally distant counterexamples. In contrast, formal XAI typically relies on feature-based abductive explanations (AXps) and contrastive explanations (CXps), which admit efficient computation and a minimal hitting-set duality. This paper bridges these perspectives by (i) characterizing closest-world counterfactual explanations explicitly as a disjunctive normal form over all minimally distant counterexample instances, and (ii) introducing conjunctive counterfactual explanations (CFXps), a subclass of counterfactual antecedents restricted to conjunctions of feature literals that guarantee prediction change. The paper establishes structural results connecting CXps, AXps and CFXps: every CXp induces an AXp for its witness instance, and every CXp together with a witness AXp induces a CFXp whose support is precisely determined by that AXp. These theoretical results yield a modular approach for computing CFXps using existing AXp/CXp machinery. Experiments on a representative set of benchmarks using tree ensembles and neural networks show that CFXp extraction is practically efficient. Compared with popular point-based counterfactual explainers, CFXps generally require no more feature changes and can guarantee the counterfactual prediction beyond a single instance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agent Skills, file-based packages of procedural knowledge that a coding agent can discover and load on demand, are now shipped by every major agent harness, yet the evidence for how much they help comes almost entirely from settings in which the right Skill is hand-picked for the task and the agent starts from a blank slate. Neither holds in deployment, where an agent chooses from whatever library happens to be installed and frequently carries memory from earlier work. We present SkillBuffBench, a benchmark and paired evaluation protocol that treats Skill access as a three-level factor (no Skill, a curated Skill, and automatic invocation from an installed library), crosses it with memory (off and on), with four agent harnesses and with multiple models (the state-of-the-art proprietary models from Anthropic, Google and OpenAI, and the state-of-the-art open-weight models from Chinese providers), every model being run under every harness, and evaluates the result on research tasks contributed by domain experts together with the Skills they use, currently in materials science and biology, with the suite extended on a rolling basis. Every run is marked by a deterministic verifier; uncertainty is reported with a paired, task-level bootstrap; and cost, agent rounds and wall-clock time are reported alongside the score rather than folded into it. This version of the paper describes the design of the benchmark, its evaluation protocol and its current tasks and Skills. The evaluation is in progress: results will be added once the runs are complete, and the task suite, results and analysis will continue to be updated on a rolling basis.

</details>

Abstract (英文): An agent resource's value depends on its backbone and companion resources and is known only after execution. Token-based generators remain tied to their training catalog, while independent resource scoring misses complementarities and reused bundles target other tasks. We formulate Task-Conditioned Agent Resource Composition (AgentComposition): jointly selecting a backbone model and a tool-skill bundle to maximize end-to-end execution utility. Our Retrieval-Grounded Bundle-Aware Agent Generation retrieves candidate resources and past configurations of similar tasks. A fine-tuned generator reads resource descriptions and recombines candidates through constrained beam search, allowing new resources without retraining. A bundle-aware critic ranks complete configurations using a large language model (LLM) teacher and historically successful configurations as execution proxies, and adapts to execution feedback when available. We evaluate reference recovery, offline execution with judged and native rewards, and online recommendation with real execution, building AgentSelect-Exec with 5,060 executed configurations. Our method recovers the complete reference configuration within ten candidates for 67.73% of AgentSelect queries, nearly twice the strongest baseline, and leads all baselines on SkillsBench. The proxy-trained critic raises the top-ranked agent's execution score from 7.58 to 8.37 out of 10; execution feedback increases it to 8.78, above the strongest baselines. Four target-domain queries suffice to adapt the generator to SkillsBench. Agents recommended online outperform the strongest baselines under both an LLM judge and native verifiers. These results support learning and transferring joint resource composition from task experience.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous shopping agents that process multimodal product listings (consisting of visual and text data) and recommend purchases on behalf of consumers. Naturally, one might assume that models with more thinking power would make higher-quality purchasing decisions. To test this belief, we ask two questions about such agents: does asking a model to think harder make it choose better products, and does the confidence it reports tell us the likelihood it may be wrong? We benchmark ten frontier models on three product-choice benchmarks built from WebShop, Amazon's SQID and Wayfair's WANDS platforms to see if LLMs with more thinking power actually make better-quality product purchase decisions. Across all three benchmarks, we found that increasing the thinking effort does not increase decision quality in a statistically meaningful way. The same change in thinking effort raises stated confidence at a statistically significant level, so extra thinking buys confidence rather than correctness. We also measure the calibration of these models (how well their reported confidence percentage matches their true correctness percentage) in these tasks and show that most models are not well-calibrated at every effort level. We also find that errors are strongly shared: when one model errs in a task, several others tend to err on that same task. Furthermore, on tasks where several models all err, a large number of them pick the same wrong product.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We describe the solver we submitted to the second round of the SOICT 2026 contest on the Integrated Passenger and Parcel Transportation problem, a selective pickup-and-delivery routing problem with time windows, open routes and heterogeneous taxis. The solver is a single-file, standard-library Python program that combines (i) a constant-time insertion test based on latest-arrival slack, (ii) greedy insertion, local search and an Adaptive Large Neighborhood Search (ALNS) with simulated-annealing acceptance, and (iii) an exact route-selection engine that enumerates, for every taxi, the best feasible route for each request set and then solves the resulting set-packing problem by branch-and-bound strengthened with route-based large neighborhood search, Lagrangian bounds and reduced-cost fixing. The engine is chosen automatically from the instance size, and an independent verifier and a watchdog guarantee that a valid answer is always printed. On the official evaluation (38 scored instances, 5-minute limit) the solver obtained a total benefit of 487,237, finished every run within 277 s and used at most 279 MB; 28 of the 38 instances terminated well before the limit.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Safety is personal: the same action can be safe for one person but unsafe for another. Yet current guardrails judge content without considering who is affected. Using medication safety, we introduce \textit{patient-conditioned medication guarding}, which predicts a safety verdict and 17 risk categories from a patient profile and prescription. We release \textbf{MedGuardBench}, a drug-disjoint benchmark of 5{,}001 scenarios across 87 medications. We find that standard guardrails rely on surface cues, such as wording and profile presence, rather than patient facts. To address this, we develop \textit{personalized reasoning guardrails} with three components. \textbf{Personalized Reasoning Distillation} links risks to relevant patient fields. \textbf{Context Unrolling (CU)} makes models check each field themselves, reducing false alarms on most backbones; controls show that providing the check does not yield the same benefit. A \textbf{rule-based reward (guided GRPO)} improves unsafe recall and complex-risk detection, including allergies and adverse reactions, on some backbones, but increases false alarms on others. Our models outperform base models and matched no-profile controls in accuracy, while reducing false alarms relative to no-profile controls. Profile removal and swapping confirm that gains come from patient information, not fine-tuning alone.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Tool-using AI agents change files, databases, access rights and public information, yet permission to call a tool does not establish whether a mistake can be undone. We present Reversibility Classes, a declared five-level vocabulary with argument-sensitive rules, conservative defaults and an explicit prior-state capture contract. The pinned implementation and evidence-linked registry cover 536 tools across 20 MCP servers. All 738 rule-case checks conform to declared expectations, while native annotation mapping differs from draft registry labels for 118 of 481 comparable tools. One AI-assisted reviewer approves 148 ratings, 75 matching the registry; this is not independent agreement. On 2,158 recorded runs, the implemented session guard gates the associated action in all 29 attributable attack-positive runs, compared with 18 for a read/write proxy, while adding nine approval points across 1,209 benign runs. Controlled file, Git, SQLite and three-browser experiments show that recovery depends on captured state, later changes, editor storage and recovery route, and expose a Git merge-state exception. The evidence supports making recovery assumptions explicit and testable. It does not establish registry-wide accuracy, reduced production harm or general recovery reliability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): An expert who corrects a concept prediction, such as a BI-RADS descriptor in breast ultrasound, expects the diagnosis to improve. Objectives that reduce the harm of such corrections are judged by harm and accuracy, yet harm also falls when a model responds less to every correction or starts from a worse uncorrected prediction, the reference for harm. We propose a correction audit, a reproducible evaluation protocol that separates these explanations: it splits each correction into harm and useful gain on identical events, separates the starting prediction from the correction response, and compares objectives with amplitude baselines matched in source harm. On three breast-ultrasound sources, worst-case correction training, evaluated from the mean-case model's start, lowers harm but gives up more useful gain in all 21 source comparisons pooled over six seeds, raising the corrected negative log-likelihood (NLL); from the same start, selecting between the objectives by harm picks the less helpful model on all three sources. On CUB, a monotonic harm penalty at its reported weight lowers path-level harm in all 80 concept sets while raising the NLL in all 80, almost entirely before any correction. At a fixed start, rescaling both responses to the same source harm reproduces the penalty's mean harm, useful gain and NLL on single-concept corrections within a prespecified margin of 0.0005 nat, although the two responses point in different directions. Lower harm therefore signals better corrections only when it is read together with useful gain, absolute risk and the start it is measured from.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Professional work involves under-specified assignments, where relevant information is spread across workplace applications and may conflict or change over time. To evaluate how well frontier agents can perform economically valuable work in such settings, we introduce ATLAS-Finance, a benchmark of 100 end-to-end investment-banking tasks across 13 simulated firms. As in real banking workflows, agents must search email, chat, calendars, notes, and files to identify requirements, gather information, reconcile conflicts/updates, and then build complex financial models. Tasks were developed by experienced bankers based on their real deal work and take them 15-30 hours to complete. Agent deliverables are evaluated with expert-authored rubrics (median: 124.5 criteria) covering financial rationale and results, spreadsheet construction, completeness, and formatting. Despite completing substantial portions of the requested work, current agents rarely produce correct deliverables. Across today's frontier models, the best ones (Claude Opus and Fable) pass fewer than 11% of tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model agents accumulate experience in memory, and how that memory evolves during interaction is as important as what it stores. Recent methods update memory online, yet both the operators that transform memory and the rules that select them remain fixed by design. We propose EvoDisco, which formulates agent memory evolution as evolutionary strategy discovery: the agent must discover which operator to apply, when to apply it, and how to instantiate it, rather than rewriting memory under a static operator schedule. A learnable policy conditions on the current memory state and recent task history, then selects and instantiates one of four semantic operators---Abstract, Refine, Bridge, and Reframe---which respectively compress related memories, decompose overloaded entries, connect weakly linked experiences, and reorganize retrieved reasoning chains. The policy is updated with a bi-level fitness. An inner signal scores each transformation using a retrieval-quality improvement regularized by memory complexity. An outer signal scores strategic efficacy over a window of past decisions, combining historical operator-utility regret, utility-prediction error, and selection diversity. Under a one-pass online plus frozen-transfer protocol on ALFWorld, WebShop, and LifelongAgentBench (OS and DB), EvoDisco improves over strong memory baselines including MemP and MemRL. The largest gains are on ALFWorld ($+8.8$ online / $+12.9$ frozen-transfer points over MemP; $+16.4$ / $+17.9$ over MemRL). Ablations attribute these gains to both fitness levels and the operator library.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) can now generate Lean proof scripts that capture the high-level structure of many mathematical arguments. However, these scripts frequently fail on complex theorems: a single missing side condition or misapplied lemma can cause the prover to abort early and discard an otherwise viable proof outline. This paper presents MathEye, a framework that converts imperfect LLM-generated proof scripts into fully verified Lean proofs by combining symbolic reasoning with minimal human interaction. At the core of MathEye is angelic proof checking, an optimistic execution model that replaces failing proof fragments with placeholders while preserving the overall structure of the argument. This optimistic check produces an angelic proof that isolates the exact proof obligations that the LLM fails to discharge. Because these unresolved obligations are often logically interdependent, asking a mathematician to address every placeholder directly is inefficient. To minimize human effort, MathEye performs residual analysis over a proof dependence graph that captures symbol-level data flow in the angelic proof. By formulating obligation selection as a MaxSAT optimization problem, MathEye selects a minimal-cost set of essential obligations whose resolution suffices to complete the entire proof. MathEye presents these essential obligations to the mathematician in ordinary mathematical language and uses the mathematician's answers to automatically reconstruct the complete Lean proof. Experiments on 30 real-world benchmarks across analysis and algebra show that MathEye solves 93.3% of the problems while reducing the number of required user interactions by 55%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Language agents rely on long-term memory to personalize responses across extended interactions, yet stored facts quietly go stale as the world changes, and an agent often learns that using memory hurt only through delayed feedback after acting on an outdated record. Before the memory store is updated, how far should that negative feedback propagate: should it suppress memory use only on future queries for that same fact (a local policy), or also across other stored facts (a global policy)? Existing memory systems implicitly fix one of these two sharing scopes at design time. We formulate this online scope choice under hidden validity and delayed feedback as memory conflict governance, and prove that neither fixed policy is sufficient on its own. We then propose RegimeCouncil, a lightweight paired-evidence router that retains both scopes as advisors and arbitrates between them online without retraining the LLM. Using revealed comparisons between answering with memory and answering without memory (the no-memory anchor), a Local Advisor tracks the latest outcome on the queried fact alone, while a Global Advisor pools recent outcomes across all stored facts. A Meta Governor follows the Local Advisor by default to avoid cross-fact interference and switches to the Global Advisor only when global pooling accumulates a strictly positive recent utility margin, guaranteeing finite-time switching under delayed feedback. Across LongMemEval update streams and real-world historical drift on TempLAMA and TAQA with Qwen3-30B-A3B and GPT-4o, RegimeCouncil outperforms the better fixed-scope policy by 5.2-7.3 points on primary disagreement episodes and across all 30 held-out settings, while ablations show that removing any of its three modules reduces accuracy by 2-7 points.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present Xing 4, a series of large language models that extends the TeleChat lineage through coordinated advances in model architecture and training infrastructure on Ascend. This report describes the design principles and systems engineering behind the series, spanning architecture selection, large-scale pretraining, and reasoning and agentic post-training. We study how sparse activation, attention design, model depth and width, and speculative decoding balance model capacity with computation, memory access, and execution overhead. Our pretraining infrastructure builds on MindSpore HyperParallel, combining topology-aware hybrid sharding, efficient distributed optimizer execution, and coordinated management of long-sequence workloads and activation memory. CPU affinity and core isolation further reduce host scheduling interference. Together, these mechanisms support the pretraining of a 285B-parameter model on 8,192 Ascend 910C cards, providing a concrete setting for the architectural and systems studies. For post-training, we introduce TeleGym, a unified agent execution platform that connects execution, evaluation, and trajectory construction to policy optimization. Disaggregated asynchronous execution, bounded oversampling, and partial rollout accommodate heterogeneous task durations while preserving trajectory continuity across policy updates. Together, these advances establish an Ascend-oriented training foundation for the continued development of Xing 4 across model scales and increasingly demanding reasoning and agent workloads.

Abstract (英文): Whole image embeddings can under represent small objects and local attributes that distinguish relevant images for localized queries. We present LoCaR, a training free framework for localized text to image retrieval with frozen vision language models. LoCaR constructs and utilizes independent proposals (SAM2) focusing on the object so as to get a more localized representation that independently encodes each region using the frozen visual encoder. We further study paired-support adaptation as an optional residual that uses matched text region exemplars without updating the underlying encoders. We also introduce LOCFG, a Visual Genome derived benchmark designed to evaluate localized retrieval for smaller and more challenging referents. Across several vision language backbones, our results show that improving the region representation itself is the main source of gain.

Abstract (英文): Many biology experiments are limited by the availability and ease of use of instruments that monitor and record them. For example, the timing of pupariation in the fruit fly Drosophila melanogaster, a widely used model organism, can provide biologically relevant information about growth regulation and is often recorded manually. Here, we present Pupavision, a small physical AI instrument built from a single-board Linux computer and a day/night camera for roughly 45 to 75 US dollars in parts. The key idea is that a frontier coding agent runs its agentic harness directly on the tiny board. The model thinks in the cloud, but the agent works on the device. The agent assists image capture, writes code to repair errors, and checks the experiment every day to keep raw data capture reliable. If recording stops, the researcher gets a ping on their mobile phone. A simple deterministic capture service records one image every ten minutes and never depends on the agent. We report a 26-day pilot in a fly incubator (about 3,580 frames), including eleven days in which the agent was logged out and capture continued, and a 51-hour outage whose cause led to the layered design. A researcher-in-the-loop workflow then produces per-vial pupariation curves by tracing selected pupae backward in time from a late reference frame. Future work will validate the assay for lifecycle tracking accuracy against expert-labeled data and expand real-time computer vision analysis. Pupavision offers a blueprint for do-it-yourself agentic instruments that put frontier intelligence on a tiny board, with deterministic code underneath. We present this as an early case study from a pilot lab deployment and contribute lessons learned toward a future where scientific domain experts are empowered by DIY agentic hardware.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-weight language models are widely observed to get worse the longer they generate. Often, attempts to fix this involve more training. At high temperature, The sampler, i.e. the rule that picks the next token out of the model's probabilities, decides whether long and agentic generation survives and/or thrives. We test 29 open-weight models from 1B to 122B parameters on ten established benchmarks plus needle retrieval out to one million tokens, covering SWE-bench Verified coding agents, long chain-of-thought reasoning, long-form writing, long structured output and long-context reading. Samplers that keep their pool of candidate tokens small and steady as temperature rises don't lose accuracy. Samplers that let in low-probability tokens fall apart. At temperature 2, coding agents running top-p, top-k or Top-H resolve 0 of 100 GitHub issues, while the same agents running top-nsigma resolve 46 to 62 and win every issue the two samplers disagree on, on three Qwen3.5 models. Over all benchmarks top-nsigma keeps a median 100% of its temperature-1 score where top-p keeps 26%. Counting how many tokens each sampler lets through explains why. In one Llama-3.1-8B story, going from temperature 1 to 2 grows top-p's pool from 4 tokens to 51,845 while top-nsigma stays at 3. Across 133 combinations of sampler setting and temperature, the typical pool size predicts judged quality (Spearman correlation of -0.77), and when we pick the pool at temperature 1 and only apply temperature afterwards, Top-H, min-p and p-less stop collapsing. At the default temperature of 1 most samplers perform about the same, We release all code and data to the public.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): From quantitative research to personal assistance, AI agents act by iteratively generating and executing code. To decouple the agent harness from code execution, many agents offload execution to dedicated systems called code sandboxes. However, existing code sandboxes suffer from slow startup. Whether they rely on custom snapshots or a single fat image, the root cause is the same: it is hard to build a compact, pre-warmed snapshot that can be shared across diverse agent-generated code while covering nearly all the artifacts (e.g., packages, datasets) it requires.
  In this work, we propose learned startup snapshots for low-latency code sandboxes. Our insight is that while it is hard for a single snapshot to be both compact and cover most workloads, a set of snapshots can achieve both. Each snapshot is small, initializes quickly, and covers a fraction of agent-generated code; together, the set covers most of it. We learn this set from a large training workload by formulating snapshot construction as an optimization problem and solving it with an approximation algorithm. Planned on 15K public and LLM-synthesized scripts, our system, Readie, reduces startup latency by 3-22$\times$ and end-to-end runtime by 1.3-3.3$\times$ compared to existing approaches at the same storage budget.

</details>

Abstract (英文): General-purpose behavior models increasingly internalize broad procedural knowledge during training, making their adaptation to new goals and skill interfaces less a problem of learning entirely new behaviors than of reconfiguring existing competence. We study this capability as reconfigurability, the ability to realize new behaviors by cost-efficiently restructuring existing components and relations under sparse governing information, where task specifications provide high-level goals and procedure specifications expose a limited set of reusable factors, leaving the concrete bindings and compositions required for execution underdetermined. We propose RecPo, a framework that represents skills task-agnostically through multiple procedure views and materializes executable skill sequences by composing stored transitions to realize task-specific changes. RecPo maintains a procedure knowledge base of skill-induced transitions, with each view exposing a reusable factor and preserving local skill connectivity. At deployment, it interprets task specifications through the same views and resolves the resulting compositional queries over the stored relations to bind skills for execution. On long-horizon tasks in an extended Franka Kitchen benchmark, RecPo achieves 91.9% ordered completion with 1.0 ms of task inference per episode across varying procedure support and task-record sparsity. Real-robot studies further demonstrate adaptation through local memory edits without task-specific retraining.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) trading agents are tested on news, but news reaches stock prices within minutes, and a benchmark that replays headlines at their publication time finds no alpha in trading on them. Money can then remain in two places, before news whose date is known, such as earnings announcements, and after bad news, if the market's fall went too far. However, studies of LLMs and earnings read documents published with or after the announcement, and studies of reversal sort monthly portfolios, so neither tells whether an agent can trade these moments after costs. In this paper, we test LLM agents at both moments on the same look-ahead-free benchmark, before 598 earnings announcements and after 409 bad-news drops of large US stocks. Every input is cut at the decision close, staged agents are compared with single calls, simple rules and a logistic regression, and every decision is made with and without the stock's name. Across five models from four vendors, no agent beat the simple bets. Before earnings, the agents mostly bought and earned what buying before every announcement earned, and after bad news, a Gemini-based agent called almost every fall an overreaction while no agent picked better dips than buying them all. The only gains came from what the models had memorized in training. Given only the ticker and the dates, a GPT-based model called the direction of 59.2% of the announcements before its training cutoff and 53.3% after it. Agent builders should therefore compare an event agent with the simple bet and test it blind after the training cutoff.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Precipitation nowcasting models suffer from errors in intensity, location, and spatial structure. Addressing these errors by retraining nowcaster backbones can be computationally expensive and requires architecture-specific modifications. To overcome these limitations, we propose CorrectCast, a decoupled residual correction framework that refines completed nowcasts while keeping the base nowcaster frozen. By operating on predicted precipitation fields, CorrectCast requires training only a lightweight corrector, reducing computational overhead while providing a unified correction interface across different nowcasting models. Conditioned on the base nowcast and historical quantitative precipitation estimation (QPE) and precipitable water vapor (PWV) observations, the corrector selects multimodal context and employs scale-aware modulation to estimate residual corrections. On the Mississippi River Basin (MRB) and contiguous United States (CONUS) datasets, CorrectCast improves six base nowcasters spanning extrapolation-based, deterministic, and generative approaches, with absolute gains in mean CSI averaging 0.0233 and 0.0290 across the six models, respectively.

</details>
