Abstract (英文): Agentic memory research has accumulated extraction, supersession, reflection, and graph synthesis on top of retrieval-augmented generation. However, we found that almost none of those primitives earn their cost in our tested memory benchmarks: we show this with BRIDGE, a deliberately minimal RAG memory with chained observation notes, hybrid BM25 with cosine retrieval, and chronological rendering, which reaches parity within bootstrap CI with the prior SOTA on LongMemEval-S and BEAM, and beats the best memory baseline on EverMemBench by 5.7-6.3 points at matched reader. It runs at ~5-7K reader-prompt tokens per query, using a single LLM call for each ingestion or query. We argue that this parity is itself the result: standard benchmarks score what memory retrieves, not what memory enables when the agent acts. To test the latter, we introduce five adversarial probes (Rule Application +-, Pattern Induction Style / Procedural, Dynamic Multiturn) judged at generation time by a cross vendor verification chain, where each query can only be solved by applying a remembered rule or pattern it never mentions. Across 5 probes x 20 scenarios, no architecture wins universally: atomic fact extractors (LangMem) win rule application, while BRIDGE's block-summary memory wins pattern induction. This result holds across four domains, three graders, and three reader families. Each design fails where the other wins, for reasons a recall benchmark cannot see: atomic-fact extractors discard the form a demonstrated pattern takes, while block-summary memory retrieves the rule but the reader does not act on it, a gap that presenting the rule more prominently largely closes. What separates memory designs is not what they retrieve but what they enable an agent to do, and that is what agentic memory should be evaluated on.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Background: Generative large language models (LLMs) offer an alternative to encoder-based clinical named entity recognition (NER), but their performance on fine-grained Japanese extraction tasks and sensitivity to generation settings remain uncertain. Methods: We compared two fine-tuned BERT-CRF baselines with nine open-weight LLMs on iCorpus, which defines 113 entity categories. The evaluation used 433 sentences from 36 test documents; 1,672 sentences were used for training. LLMs were evaluated using zero-shot prompting with and without JSON Schema constraints and three-seed QLoRA fine-tuning. Fine-tuned models were assessed under different output-token limits, including 900 and larger model-specific limits of 2,600 or 4,096 tokens. Performance was measured using sentence-level label-surface matching with relaxed and exact-surface F1. Results: The highest zero-shot relaxed F1 was 0.337, compared with 0.836-0.841 for the BERT baselines. At the larger output limits, all eight fine-tuned LLMs with at least 13B total parameters exceeded both BERT baselines (relaxed F1, 0.882-0.914; exact-surface F1, 0.840-0.880). Across three-seed means, increasing the output limit improved relaxed F1 by 0.052-0.085, alongside higher recall and fewer length-limited outputs. JSON Schema constraints changed relaxed F1 by -0.014 to +0.008 among seven models outside the two reasoning-oriented configurations, while medical-general model pairs showed no consistent advantage for medical specialization. Conclusions: On this historical Japanese case-report benchmark, task-specific fine-tuning combined with adequate generation capacity was associated with stronger LLM extraction performance. Output-token limits materially affected comparisons with BERT, whereas enforcing output structure alone did not overcome low zero-shot accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Attribution-guided pruning scores components by their causal contribution and protects the important ones, and it is increasingly applied to compress reasoning language models without destroying their chain of thought. Its reliability rests on an unexamined assumption: that reasoning importance can be faithfully measured. Across four small reasoning models, we show this measurement is fragile in three ways, each of which changes pruning decisions. First, scoring final-answer tokens, the natural choice, saturates in every chain-of-thought model we test (answer-token negative log-likelihood, NLL, of $10^{-4}$--$10^{-3}$) because the model states its answer inside its own reasoning; guiding head pruning with this target costs 27.6 and 32.2 accuracy points relative to scoring reasoning tokens on the two models we power with five seeds, while perplexity barely moves and even reverses sign at 7B. Second, causal importance is not localizable to single units: single-neuron effects sit at the measurement floor, their cross-split ranking is noise, and they cancel: a group's ablation effect disagrees in sign with the summed effects of its members in 90\% of groups. Reproducible importance emerges only at groups of $\sim 100$ neurons. Third, contrastively isolated ``reasoning-specific'' importance is not separable from generic importance, and protecting it is worse than protecting raw importance. We additionally pre-registered the hypothesis that distillation-induced concentration of importance predicts the pruning cost; it is not supported, and since our grid confounds concentration with scale, we report it as unresolved rather than reversed. The result is a short set of measurement rules: score reasoning tokens, attribute at coarse granularity, and evaluate with accuracy rather than perplexity.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Coding agents repeatedly send their conversation history to a language model. Adding useful code may reduce search, while removing old tool outputs may shorten later prompts. But do these changes reduce the cost of solving a task? We study this as a measurement problem in more than 20,000 runs of open models on SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench and Loc-Bench. We account separately for uncached input, cached input and output tokens. Three findings emerge. First, in our main evidence-package experiments, retrieved code reduces read and search commands by 18--34%, but increases cost by 33--42% when cached input costs one tenth of uncached input, without a measurable improvement in resolution. Second, removing old observations can either save money or increase cost: the same approach saves 19--36% without a cache discount but increases cost by 38--116% with a tenth-price cache. Third, shorter prompts do not guarantee cheaper runs because agents may respond by taking more steps. We also find that concurrent experiments can share cached prefixes and distort cost comparisons. These results show why context-management methods should be evaluated using both token prices and the agent's subsequent behavior.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-agent delegation lets LLM systems distribute evidence collection across workers with separate contexts. This creates a consistency challenge: facts retrieved by workers may be missing from the evidence available to the lead for a joint conclusion. We introduce a benchmark and evidence-flow profiler for studying multi-agent memory inconsistency through controlled factual conflicts in Harvey LAB document-review tasks. Pooled across three models with comparable generation spending, requiring delegation increases retrieval of both conflicting claims from 50.2% to 68.7% relative to single-agent execution, yet conflict detection falls from 16.2% to 13.2%. Within this setting, workers report both claims for only 35.2% of retrieved pairs, and pooled matched comparisons show higher detection than single-agent execution when one worker reads both claims but lower detection when the claims are split across workers. Worker-to-lead handoffs are a central target for improving conflict detection in multi-agent LLMs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Objective. Paper-based assessments are common in patient care, but handwriting and scanning artifacts make these forms difficult to process at scale. This study proposes a modular, multimodal LLM-based system with human oversight for extracting fields and scores. We conducted a qualitative analysis of the use of LLMs for hand-drawing analysis.
  Materials and Methods. The system processes cognitive assessments (i.e., MMSE, MoCA, Mini-Cog) and the NICHQ Vanderbilt Assessment using two modules: 1) a form classifier that assigns a scanned page to a form type, followed by a corresponding 2) score extractor that extracts fields and scores into a schema. A human-in-the-loop design utilizing dual-model validation engages human annotators when two LLMs disagree, limiting manual review to challenging pages only.
  Results. End-to-end accuracy for cognitive assessments was 88%, with most form types exceeding 90%. For the NICHQ Vanderbilt, overall accuracy reached 97.5%, with all form types above 96%. The human-in-the-loop design further improved cognitive assessment accuracy to 97.5% while reducing manual work by 63.8%. In the Mini-Cog Clock Drawing task, qualitative analysis showed that 62.3% of all extraction errors stemmed from ambiguous drawing, in which cases the LLM provided reasonable readings.
  Discussion. Compared to current manual data extraction, our LLM-based system reduces processing time by 10 to 20 times and costs by over 12 times. The modular design allows seamless expansion to additional form types.
  Conclusion. Our system provides an accurate, scalable, and cost-efficient solution for structuring data trapped in handwritten paper forms for clinical decision support.

</details>

Abstract (英文): RLVF assumes the verifier evaluates the quantity of interest. In genetic-circuit design, a topological verifier can establish that a program is a valid circuit computing the target function, whereas ranking its physical performance requires a simulator. We measure fidelity regret, the reference simple regret incurred when a cheap simulator selects the design, over a 21,362-design combination subspace spanning 5 measured gate libraries and 3 organisms. Exact ordered-assignment checks retain positive regret in every tested cell while shifting magnitudes in both directions. Mean normalized regret is 0.369 over 72 cells and only 6 have zero regret. The discrepancy depends strongly on the reference axis: the yeast library SC1C1G1T1 has mean regret 0.113 for cytometry quality and 0.801 for growth burden, and every evaluated library--circuit-size pair has positive burden regret. Topological verification ties all valid gate assignments, while an oracle whitelist subject to retaining at least 25% of the design space leaves positive regret in 30 of 52 cells. Design-space optimization and feedback-guided search with frozen language models reach the high-regret regions; on a matched zero-regret control, additional optimization instead reduces regret. At a matched modeled-cost budget, mitigation is budget- and library-dependent. An in-sample fixed rule spending $α{=}0.34$ of the budget re-scoring top cheap-simulator designs achieves mean excess regret 0.016, compared with 0.305 for cheap-only and 0.034 for reference-only selection, improving on cheap-only in 330 of 360 cells. More broadly, any workflow optimized through a simulator or digital twin can satisfy its verifier yet perform poorly on the intended physical objective. Fidelity regret provides a transferable template for detecting this gap before optimization and comparing budget-aware remedies across scientific domains.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Ensuring robust safety alignment of large language models (LLMs) is increasingly difficult as adversarial attacks evolve and outpace alignment pipelines built on pre-collected data. Existing co-evolutionary frameworks let the defender chase a moving attacker, yet their training dynamics expose two compounding pathologies: \emph{attack strategy collapse}, where the attacker overfits to a narrow set of high-reward rewrites, and \emph{defense adversarial forgetting}, where the defender loses competence against earlier attacks as the attack distribution drifts. Existing remedies are partial: attacker-side diversity rewards score textual novelty rather than strategy novelty, and defender-side replay relies on discrete judge scores that ignore estimation uncertainty, response stochasticity, and defender non-stationarity. We introduce \textbf{\method}, a diversity-driven adversarial co-evolution framework that targets both pathologies jointly. On the attacker side, \method couples an explicit two-dimensional strategy space (risk category $\times$ attack style) with a \emph{normalized marginal coverage gain} reward, providing a bounded, non-vanishing exploration signal at the strategy layer. On the defender side, \method maintains a unified \emph{Bayesian adversarial replay pool} whose Beta--Bernoulli threat posteriors are refreshed by time decay and sampled via Thompson sampling, anchoring defender training to an evolving threat landscape. Across standard safety, automated-attacker, and general-capability benchmarks, \method improves robustness to out-of-distribution attacks while preserving general capabilities, and yields broader attacker strategy coverage.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate seasonal precipitation forecasts help societies prepare for drought, flooding, and crop failure months in advance. While data-driven methods have made rapid progress in weather and subseasonal forecasting, operational seasonal precipitation prediction still relies on numerical ensemble systems. These systems are computationally expensive and exhibit biases and drift that increase with lead time, motivating more efficient data-driven alternatives. Yet this transition faces three linked challenges: comparing data-driven forecasts with operational ones requires (1) a global benchmark that does not exist, scoring them requires (2) metrics that capture operational priorities beyond anomaly correlation (ACC) and RMSE, and training them must (3) rely on a single, short observational record. To address the first two challenges, we introduce \BenchName, the first such benchmark, covering monthly precipitation forecasts 1--5 months ahead. It pairs global $1^\circ$ satellite precipitation with oceanic and atmospheric predictors, includes seven operational centers, and provides 310,944 simulated months from 52 climate models for training. Beyond standard scores, we introduce two operationally motivated metrics for anomaly strength and monthly rain-belt position. To address the third challenge, we develop \MODEL, which learns from simulations what the record lacks and transfers it to Earth through three modules: SimForced aligns the climate background, SimMemory transfers a history encoder pretrained on simulations, and SimAnalog supplements Earth labels with simulated analog outcomes. Extensive experiments show that \MODEL{} improves ACC, CRPS, anomaly strength, and rain-belt position by 12.0\%, 3.6\%, 2.9\%, and 0.3\% over the strongest operational or data-driven model, opening a path from simulated worlds to Earth system prediction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Statistical downscaling restores the regional detail behind flooding and crop stress, but diffusion and flow-matching generators can produce sharp fields that break the conservation laws of their coarse input. Physics-Constrained Rectified Flow (PC-RF) is a conditional flow-matching downscaler. Its training penalties target wind divergence, negative precipitation, and mass drift from the coarse total, and a projection after sampling removes the last two exactly. On one corrupted synthetic field with no trained network, the projection drives the Non-negativity Violation Rate (NVR) from 0.236 and the Mass-Conservation Error (MCE) from 0.184 to zero, and the penalties lower the Divergence Error (DE) by about 30%. On ERA5 the projection gives PC-RF and RF-base, the penalty-free rectified-flow baseline, an NVR of exactly zero and an MCE of zero up to float32 rounding. The penalties add no perceptual gain that we can separate from training budget or run-to-run variation. At a matched budget on 2019-2021, PC-RF has no edge over RF-base (SSIM 0.939 vs. 0.942, PSNR 31.81 vs. 31.79, CRPS 0.771 vs. 0.772). Our unprojected CorrDiff reimplementation beats both flow models on PSNR (28.38) and CRPS (1.092) on 2020, yet 45% of its ensemble-mean precipitation cells are negative. On ERA5 a sign error in the divergence operator made that penalty and DE act on a deformation-like term. The fine reference is interpolated from ERA5 and accumulates precipitation over a longer window, so the perceptual scores cannot establish downscaling skill. NVR and MCE need one pass and no fine reference, so we argue they should run first as pass-or-fail checks, with DE as a diagnostic.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Transverse feedback linearization (TFL) achieves path following by exactly linearizing the error dynamics, but its controller is undefined on a singular set, such as zero speed for a unicycle or zero thrust for a quadrotor. The constrained region of attraction, the set of initial states from which the closed loop converges to the path without violating state or regularity constraints, determines where the controller can be safely activated. Exploiting the exact linear error dynamics, we show that this region is the largest forward invariant subset of the admissible set. We then decompose its state-constrained Zubov value function into an analytically known quadratic cost-to-go and a safety value function. We learn only the latter, by fitted value iteration without infinite-horizon trajectory labels. The resulting estimate combines an inner layer verified by interval branch and bound with a learned outer layer tested on samples. On a unicycle following a circle and a sinusoid and on a planar quadrotor, it covers on average 72.0%, 83.4% and 19.1% of the simulated region of attraction. In comparison, the quadratic cost-to-go and two physics-informed neural network (PINN) solvers of Zubov's equation cover at most 11.3%, 4.0% and 0.0% on average. Code and animations are available at https://gradslab.github.io/roa_tfl

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large-language-model agents now close the industrial predictive-maintenance loop, reading sensor windows, retrieving procedures and dispatching work orders. Two attack channels meet there: injected text in a retrieved document can hijack the decision, and a bounded sensor perturbation can corrupt the remaining-useful-life estimate behind it. Recent agent defenses police data provenance and tool privilege but cannot tell whether a prognostic quantity is physically plausible. ConfGuard is a reference monitor with a physics-grounded policy. Its conformal monitor tests each prediction, anchored to the asset's admitted history, against degradation physics: capped remaining life never rises and falls at most one cycle per cycle. Calibrating its threshold over whole asset trajectories lets the operator set the expected share of a clean asset's windows the running monitor escalates. A physics-policy validator checks the agent's maintenance-urgency decision against the decision a deterministic policy derives from the same interval. On all 10,196 C-MAPSS test windows the monitor cuts a covert attacker's reach on healthy assets from 0.529 to 0.046 and closes suppression of failing assets (0.151 to 0), escalating 0.027 of clean windows against a 0.05 target, while the fixed physical range it replaces leaves both attacks open. Behind a Qwen3-14B planner the composed monitors hold urgency-class injection to 0.00 and the universal sensor attack to 0.12, against 0.29 and 0.54 for a defended prompt. An adaptive attacker defeats a Mahalanobis detector on 0.504 of windows and adversarial training on 0.485. We report an attack class the projection misses.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Speculative decoding (SD) accelerates large language model (LLM) inference, but its optimal configuration depends jointly on workload characteristics, GPU hardware, and available memory. For example, on the RTX~5090, the state-of-the-art speculative drafter DFlash achieves $3.50\times$ the throughput of autoregressive (AR) decoding at low concurrency, but only $0.76\times$ at high concurrency with short outputs. We present SpecSearch, a hardware-aware search engine that jointly optimizes decoding strategy, draft length, and precision across workload--GPU pairs. Its analytical cost model captures memory and compute constraints to identify feasible configurations and narrow the search space for throughput optimization. Guided by this model, calibration measurements are collected from multiple GPUs. Using the measurements, a Jev-based predictor learns to select high-throughput decoding configurations on held-out GPUs without requiring deployment-GPU calibration. The predictor is parameter-efficiently trained on calibration data, while adapting a frozen Qwen3-4B using LoRA adapters and a linear correction layer. Across NVIDIA RTX~5090, H100, L40 and AMD MI300X GPUs, SpecSearch with full calibration achieves an average of $2.01\times$ the throughput of AR and $1.36\times$ the throughput of SD, both served by vLLM. The proposed Jev-based predictor closely matches this search performance with $1.30\times$ throughput improvement over SD with vLLM, while eliminating the need for held-out GPU calibration. Additionally, it retains $97.36\%$ of the throughput achieved by SpecSearch that utilizes full calibration.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In on-policy distillation (OPD), response completion, supervision delivery, and parameter updates need not share the same granularity. We develop streaming OPD by decoupling these three clocks: a response repeatedly delivers newly generated segments while generation continues, retaining the full causal prefix at each delivery and computing loss only on new positions. A consumption protocol then organizes supervision budgets, position selection, staleness handling, and update scheduling, allowing control over quantity, coverage, and version lag across training stages. In GSM8K experiments distilling Qwen3-4B into Qwen3-0.6B with VERL, supervised-budget matching reduces the early gap between best test accuracies over seven common candidates from 4.55 to 2.42 points. Synchronous continuation from the same checkpoint reaches 73.39% best test accuracy over seven common candidates at u=384,...,576, versus 74.30% synchronous and 71.95% budget-matched (differences -0.91 and -2.35 points); an unpaired extended record reaches 76.12%. Freshness filtering and disagreement-overlap selection improve local peaks but do not establish sustained advantages. A system prototype shows lower data-supply waiting times without establishing an equal-update speedup. Quality recovery and supply-side benefits are assessed separately; end-to-end gains must account for continuation cost.

Abstract (英文): Whether a foundation model succeeds as an autonomous agent depends as much on its runtime software as on its weights. This runtime, the agent harness, manages context and memory, steers multi-turn control, executes tool actions in sandboxes, and verifies work before stopping. Because models differ in how they use tools and how they fail, a static harness tuned for one model or task often stalls, wastes tokens, or regresses on another. This bottleneck has driven a shift from prompt tuning to self-improving agent harnesses: systems that inspect their own execution traces, separate runtime bugs from model limits, and rewrite their own code. We present a unifying architectural and design framework for self-improving agent harnesses. The framework pairs four editable runtime components (context and state, agent control, action interfaces, and verification and recovery) with a four-stage loop that diagnoses failures, proposes code patches, validates candidates, and deploys updates. Using this framework, we show how optimizers trace errors across long trajectories and discover reusable runtime mechanisms such as pre-stop verifiers, loop breakers, and persistent memory. We further show why full-harness rewrites overfit or break across modules, and how modular updates with validation gates generalize across models while bounding token cost. To keep self-modification safe, we establish practical defenses against recurring failure modes including prompt bloat, benchmark gaming, and evaluator tampering. Finally, experiments on SWE-bench Lite validate this framework, showing that gated modular evolution outperforms prompt reflection and full-harness rewrites across models and repositories at a fraction of the token cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) has powered landmark successes in reinforcement learning, yet its gains in LLM reasoning can remain modest despite substantial search effort. We identify an overlooked bottleneck: what MCTS counts. Standard UCT assigns independent visit counts to textual branches, allowing different formulations of the same reasoning strategy to repeatedly receive exploration bonuses. Our theoretical analysis shows that this count fragmentation can impair exploration efficiency, as quantified by increased cumulative regret. To address this problem, we introduce Learned Pseudo-Count MCTS (LPC-MCTS), which changes only the exploration statistic in UCT. A lightweight Coin-Flipping Network head on the policy's hidden representations estimates representation coverage, enabling related branches to share exploration evidence. Across 5 mathematical-reasoning and 7 search benchmarks, LPC-MCTS consistently outperforms strong MCTS baselines. Holding each LPC-MCTS-trained policy fixed, replacing standard MCTS counts at inference improves average mean@16 accuracy across 5 mathematical benchmarks by 3.79 percentage points on Qwen2.5-7B and 2.71 points on Qwen3-4B. Applying LPC-MCTS during both training and inference yields gains of 4.96 and 4.37 points over standard MCTS, respectively. Together, our theory and experiments show that changing what MCTS counts can substantially improve its exploration efficiency and reasoning performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Editing a building information model in the Industry Foundation Classes (IFC) schema requires propagating changes to dependent elements while preserving unrelated entities. Existing language-model agents for IFC editing use prompted models without learning from edit outcomes. Target-file comparisons do not specify which alternative edits are allowed or which unrelated entities must remain unchanged. We present IfcGym, a CodeAct environment in which a language model edits IFC through executable code and four checkers verify validity relative to the input model, intent, dependency and preservation against a task specification. Their conjunction is strict success. IfcEdit distils verified teacher episodes into a Qwen3.5-9B student through supervised fine-tuning (SFT), then applies Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with strict success as the reward. On IfcEditBench, 1,990 editing tasks on 13 held-out real projects, the final student reaches 86.78% strict success, from 79.15% after SFT. The paired confidence interval for the difference from its teacher includes zero; the same recipe on a 27B model reaches 91.76% and exceeds the teacher. On the public benchmarks BIM-Edit and BIBIMBAP the final student scores 55.19 and 54.50. On IfcEditBench, the students outperform all prompted models except the teacher under matched tool access; both evaluated frameworks also score lower. Rewarding all four checkers yields 4.83 points more strict success than rewarding intent and validity alone.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generative retrieval (GR) predicts catalog entries as short semantic identifiers (SIDs). Trie-constrained beam search keeps these identifiers valid, but wide beams expose two serving costs: vocabulary-wide score tensors that are mostly discarded, and repeated attention over the same prompt. We present Trie-Head, a model-native output interface that stores the identifier trie as validated GPU tensors. Compared with a Python custom logit processor (CLP), it reduces CPU--GPU coordination and memory traffic by avoiding Python constraint callbacks and vocabulary-wide candidate buffers. It scores compact codebook candidates and streams the full-vocabulary normalizer, preserving the original model's valid-token scores in real arithmetic without retraining. Shared-context attention complements this output path by reusing prompt key/value blocks across beams while preserving their private decode tails. We implement both mechanisms in SGLang and evaluate candidate sourcing for LinkedIn Hiring Assistant and qualification retrieval for the Qualification Retrieval Platform. The combined system improves closed-loop throughput by up to 5.57x over the CLP baseline and up to 2.63x over BF16 FlashRec at a common beam width of 200 on the Qualification Retrieval Platform workload.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Despite advances in LLM-based table reasoning, most studies focus on single-table settings. Multi-table QA requires multi-hop reasoning over complex table relationships and evidence dispersed across long contexts, often without verifiable intermediate traces. To address these challenges, we introduce EviDA, an evidence-centric dual-agent framework comprising an Evidence Construction Agent and a Reasoning and Answering Agent. The Evidence Construction Agent generates and executes table-manipulation programs over complete tables to produce question-specific evidence and verifiable intermediate traces, simplifying subsequent reasoning. The Reasoning and Answering Agent then performs multi-hop reasoning over this evidence to derive final answers. We further introduce EviDARL, which combines agent-specific fine-grained rewards with parameter-efficient alternating reinforcement learning to improve collaboration between the agents. By optimizing each agent in turn while holding the other fixed, EviDARL enables bidirectional adaptation between evidence construction and answer reasoning. Experiments on MMQA and TQA-Bench demonstrate that EviDARL substantially outperforms competitive open-model and multi-table reasoning baselines under constrained model and training budgets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Ride-hailing platforms reposition idle vehicles to improve spatial supply-demand balances. This problem is prediction-driven and multistage: at each stage, the platform forecasts future demand, executes current decisions, and uses the resulting state to initialize the next stage. We formulate a multistage linear program (LP) with a macroscopic fundamental diagram (MFD) linking accumulation to trip completion. We then develop a multistage contextual optimization approach that uses decision performance to train the prediction model. The learning problem combines multiple coupled stages, context that depends on earlier decisions, and predictions that affect the objective, the constraint matrix, and the right-hand side of the LP. We derive the exact gradient, which has three parts: parameters change forecasts and hence decisions; earlier decisions change the current decision through the inherited state; and this state also changes forecasts as context. An equivalent adjoint uses reverse vector-Jacobian products, avoiding full solution and state-parameter Jacobians. Finally, forward propagation solves many stage LPs one after another, which makes training slow. We propose a parallel block evaluation algorithm that solves blocks of consecutive stages in parallel and repeatedly feeds each block output to the next block. We prove that the algorithm recovers the serial trajectory within a finite number of passes and that its gradient error remains bounded when it stops early. On New York City ride-hailing data, multistage contextual optimization yields 7.7% to 9.1% more business benefit than the same predictor trained by mean squared error, outperforms all compared baselines from five paradigms, and even outperforms realized demand fed to the same policy. With eight workers, parallel block evaluation shortens training by a factor of 1.86 without loss of decision performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We consider the problem of evaluating AI agents undertaking investment-research tasks, where agents interact with external information sources and computational tools to produce financial analyses and research reports. An important challenge is that the quality of an agent's final output does not necessarily reflect the quality of its execution process, while the reliability of the resulting assessment also depends on the validity of the evaluation procedure. Furthermore, investment-research tasks differ considerably in their requirements for numerical accuracy, information retrieval, financial analysis and supporting evidence, making it difficult to assess agent performance using a uniform set of criteria. We propose a domain-oriented evaluation framework that distinguishes outcome quality from execution quality and accommodates these differences through task-specific criteria, deterministic validators and independent large language model judges. We also introduce an evaluation-validity mechanism that examines the integrity of evaluation inputs, reference information, supporting evidence and grading outputs, with expert review used to resolve identified deficiencies and significant grading disagreements. Numerical results show that the proposed framework can distinguish differences in agent performance across evaluation dimensions, while identifying potential reliability issues in the evaluation process. The associated implementation and evaluation materials will be made available on GitHub.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Complex health conversations require answers grounded in individual information and currently valid evidence. We evaluate health context learning: acquiring, integrating, and applying relevant context during inference with fixed model parameters to answer health questions and respond appropriately to changes in facts, evidence, or task goals. To our knowledge, Chinese-language medical benchmarks have yet to systematically combine explicit contextual difficulties, controlled case variations, and member-specific medical criteria for open-ended answers. We introduce HealthCLBench, comprising 328 controlled clusters, 1,103 cases, and 4,931 rubric criteria across medical consultation and everyday health management. Members share backgrounds while evidence, patient states, or task goals change; each has its own medical scoring requirements. A 70-person medical team annotates and reviews the simulated cases and their rubrics. Human review of 700 full-context responses from the original evaluation yields 90.5% pooled agreement with the original automated judgments at the rubric-item level. We report full-context and query-only results for seven general-purpose models and Ant Afu. Across the eight systems, full- query differences range from 11.83 to 31.76 case points and from 10.87 to 38.97 geometric cluster points. GPT-6-Astra leads both full-context metrics at 75.03 and 60.42, followed by Claude-Opus-5-5 and Ant Afu. Cross-condition differences describe score changes under the reported evaluation settings. Response comparisons reveal failures in checking historical information, expressing uncertainty when warranted, and ensuring recommendations respect required conditions. The resource supports model comparison and targeted follow-up evaluation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Changing the holding reference during a fixed-duration wait can reverse the ranking of visual recovery methods. We introduce AuditEmbodied, a paired execution and trajectory evaluation framework that independently varies holding references and waiting duration while keeping recovery executors frozen. A 960-execution factorial on 20 initialization seeds compares CAD-feedback and landmark-based recovery from identical post-wait physical states within each holding and waiting condition. Protected completion requires successful recovery under the declared execution constraints. At a 1 s wait with registered wrist references, their protected completion counts change from 6/40 versus 19/40 under registered holding to 34/40 versus 22/40 under position-only holding. Trajectory analysis localizes the main separation to grasp formation: small observation-time target residuals precede large tracking errors. Joint diagnostics and controlled interventions characterize persistent tracking constraints. On fresh seeds, gradual position reanchoring improves CAD recovery's long-wait completion by 42.5 percentage points and reduces mean complete recovery time by 6.06 s relative to registered holding. Additional evaluations with pretrained policies, handover, and a mass-matched geometry characterize holding-reference sensitivity across executors and physical regimes. These findings establish holding reference as an explicit evaluation condition for comparing visual recovery performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AutoE2E is an open-source end-to-end driving model developed by the Autoware Foundation Robotaxi Working Group. It introduces a temporal bird's-eye-view (BEV) architecture that combines multi-camera perception, HD-map information and route conditioning to predict future vehicle motion. The evaluated model uses a pretrained camera-BEV backbone, temporal feature fusion and a lightweight recurrent trajectory planner. It contains approximately 79.9 million parameters, of which approximately 1.45 million are trainable in the evaluated configuration. The v1.0 release provides model checkpoints, training and evaluation code, and DataModelConsole, a dashboard for visualizing and inspecting model experiments. On the KITScenes validation benchmark, the fine-tuned checkpoint achieves an average displacement error (ADE) of 0.745 m and a final displacement error (FDE) of 2.020 m at a three-second prediction horizon. The paper also reports preliminary real-vehicle research conducted with the Technical University of Munich's EDGAR platform, alongside ongoing collaboration with the Karlsruhe Institute of Technology. AutoE2E v1.0 is an early open research release. Its quantitative evaluation is open-loop, and the model has not yet demonstrated validated closed-loop autonomous driving. The release is intended to provide the autonomous-driving community with an inspectable baseline for further research, experimentation and collaborative development.

Abstract (英文): Autonomous systems choose actions whose outcomes are uncertain and are subject to time constraints. Probabilistic timed automata (PTAs) model exactly this, and verifying one answers a question about the system's decision-making: the maximum probability of reaching a goal, or the minimum probability of reaching a hazard, that a controller can guarantee. Verification normally works over symbolic states, each pairing a discrete location with a region of clock values, and its difficulty is that the best behavior generally varies within such a region. The PRISM verifier's most capable PTA method responds by splitting regions until each is uniform, rebuilding the transitions that reach them and propagating the split backward through the state graph of a stochastic game, a cascade of graph reconstruction that partitions regions the property never depends on. We present SYMAR, which instead makes the value function symbolic: each symbolic state carries a function from sub-regions of its clock region to probability bounds, so refinement is the appearance of new sub-regions in that function, and previously generated states are never split nor their incoming transitions reconstructed. Both sources of PTA nondeterminism, the timing and the choice of actions, are captured implicitly by the preimage computation that produces those sub-regions. Heuristic forward search then bounds how much refinement is done at all, expanding an And-Or graph from the initial state along the most promising behaviors so that effort is confined to the property-relevant fragment, guided by bounds and preferred actions from a single unrefined run of the existing method; a partial-refinement backup keeps the otherwise combinatorial sub-region count lean, and every stored interval provably contains the true probability. On the PRISM PTA benchmarks, whose subproblems recur in autonomous systems (distributed agreement under contention, leader election, and self-configuration without central coordination), SYMAR is up to 108x faster, solves an instance on which the existing method exhausts the time limit, and is markedly less sensitive to the deadline constant, resolving one instance in a 37-node search graph, unchanged as the deadline triples, where the existing method needs 86,380 abstract states and 73 refinement rounds.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Robotic applications often rely on long-running operations, such as navigation, manipulation, and inspection, which in ROS~2 are commonly implemented as actions. Unlike topics and services, actions involve a structured lifecycle comprising goal submission and acceptance, feedback, status updates, result retrieval, and cancellation. Existing versions of ROSMonitoring support topics and services, but do not reconstruct this lifecycle or expose action-level events to a runtime-verification oracle. In this paper, we extend ROSMonitoring with runtime monitoring and enforcement for ROS~2 actions. Actions are declared directly in the monitoring configuration and automatically instrumented through the underlying ROS~2 communication endpoints. The generated monitor reconstructs action-level events, correlates them through goal identifiers, maintains the state of active goals, and translates violations into protocol-aware enforcement operations. These include goal rejection, cancellation, message suppression, and protocol-compliant responses generated directly by the monitor when the original communication cannot be forwarded. We evaluate the implementation through controlled stress tests that vary the number of action invocations, workload size, and client–server topology. The results show approximately linear growth in monitoring cost with repeated action invocations under a fixed workload, while larger action workloads and a greater number of configured client–server pairs introduce additional overhead. These results characterise the scaling behaviour of the current implementation under the tested sequential workloads while highlighting the main performance limitations of the current synchronous implementation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Fast System-1 models enable responsive actions but struggle with complex or unfamiliar tasks. Large language model (LLM)-based System-2 provides strong reasoning capabilities, yet repeated invocation incurs substantial latency and computational costs. To bridge this gap, we propose JevRSI, a System-1/System-2 collaboration framework that transforms LLM reasoning into transferable decision support through recursive harness improvement. A Jev-style System-1 model enables low-latency action selection, while an asynchronous System-2 LLM develops a modular harness. The harness integrates procedures, state memory, and world models to provide decision contexts spanning the past, present, and future. Utility-driven retrieval selects modules based on both contextual relevance and their utility on previous tasks. Environmental feedback guides targeted revisions and enables recursive self-improvement. Experiments across three datasets show that JevRSI consistently improves decision quality, matching or surpassing LLM agents in several settings. It preserves low-latency execution with favorable performance-cost trade-offs. Moreover, System-2 development interventions decrease as the harness evolves, amortizing System-2 costs through continuous improvement. Our project is available at https://github.com/usail-hkust/JevRSI.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Omnimodal large language models (Omni-LLMs) integrate audio, video, and text for multimodal reasoning, yet remain vulnerable to cross-modal hallucinations, where one modality improperly influences predictions about another. Existing training-free decoding methods mitigate these errors through modality perturbation or relevance-based weighting. However, due to interference from irrelevant modality cues during fusion and difficulty detecting shared errors, these methods may suppress correct unimodal predictions and allow erroneous consensus to persist. To address these limitations, we propose Provenance-Aware Consensus Decoding (PACD), a training-free framework for mitigating cross-modal hallucinations. PACD comprises two complementary paths based on whether the branches reach consensus. For non-consensus cases, Relevance-Guided Candidate Arbitration identifies task-relevant unimodal evidence and selectively arbitrates between predictions using alternative candidates. For consensus cases, Provenance-Based Consensus Recovery leverages the outranking principle to evaluate the consistency and strength of layer-wise modality evidence for alternative answers, selectively resolving erroneous consensus under confidence constraints. Extensive experiments on four Omni-LLMs and five benchmarks across two tasks demonstrate the superiority of PACD. Compared to existing methods, PACD achieves up to 27.6% relative improvement over standard decoding across CMM, AVHBench, and SVHalluc for cross-modal hallucination mitigation, and an average relative improvement of 7.7% over best baseline across OmniVideoBench and FutureOmni for general audio-visual reasoning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Integrating IoT devices into household systems offers enhanced control over living environments. However, as these devices become pervasive in critical sectors such as healthcare, transportation, and home automation, they expose inter-rule vulnerabilities that may lead to privacy breaches, data theft, and physical harm. Traditional fuzz testing methods, which often operate in simulated environments assuming ideal human decisions, fail to account for the complexities of human interactions that can influence device operations in smart homes, potentially leading to unforeseen consequences. To reveal the impact of real human decisions and discover unexpected inter-rule vulnerabilities, we introduce an enhanced version of IoT Fuzz for smart homes by integrating large language models to emulate human decision-making processes. Our novel approach adds a layer of realism and unpredictability to the testing environment, more accurately capturing the diverse and dynamic nature of human interactions in smart home settings.

Abstract (英文): Organizations increasingly ask AI systems what will happen if they change how they operate: which work to automate, which team to resize, what a voice agent will do to waiting times. We introduce RehearsalBench, a benchmark that measures whether AI models can diagnose an operation, choose an intervention, and forecast its consequences. Each model is placed in a simulated bank contact center fitted to real call-level records, sees only what a manager would see, and submits a plan and forecasts that are then played out across 200 seeded futures and three plausible versions of the organization. Decisions are scored in dollars at the stakeholders' stated valuations; forecasts are scored on the effect of each change relative to doing nothing. Ten models made 280 attempts across 14 organizations. The best model, GPT-6 Astra, improved on doing nothing by 6.8% of the no-change cost and made 77% less forecast error than a forecaster who always predicts no effect. On average, every model forecast the direction of every specified effect correctly, but models erred on the size of the same effects in the same direction, overstating a voice agent's effect on repeat contacts about five-fold. No model ever recommended holding off when doing nothing was the right choice. To test external validity, twelve models forecast 33 real organizational changes with known outcomes. Under verdict rules fixed in advance, the benchmark's forecast ranking agreed with the real-world ranking (Spearman $ρ= 0.92$, 95% lower bound 0.50). We report the result alongside an inconclusive first test, the analysis choices made after outcomes were seen, and evidence that part of the agreement reflects how well models size their uncertainty.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Language models are increasingly being used as agents capable of planning and executing projects, as well as refining their actions through feedback. This shift raises a question: can current agents complete projects like real scientists? To examine this question, we introduce SciWorkBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents' abilities on real science tasks across biology, chemistry, materials science, and physics. SciWorkBench is built from peer-reviewed literature or expert-contributed research scenarios. We also propose a unified scientific analysis framework validated by domain experts, which can categorize most tasks in SciWorkBench. Each task covers one or more stages of this framework. We evaluate up-to-date models within the Claude Code and Codex agent frameworks under a unified execution and grading protocol. The best-performing agent achieves a mean score of 57.38, indicating that current agents still struggle with realistic scientific workflows. Our failure analysis identifies three recurring weaknesses at the workflow level: difficulty translating scientific knowledge into task-specific procedures, weak integration of evidence across different modalities, and limited capacity for scientific self-correction. These findings highlight the importance of workflow-based evaluation for measuring progress toward reliable AI systems for scientific research.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present DARWIN, a geospatial foundation model for marine intelligence. Recent Earth-observation foundation models have concentrated on terrestrial domains. The marine realm poses distinct challenges: missingness, multi-modal physical and biogeochemical variables, sparse labels, and dynamics that are transient, multi-scale, and non stationary over time. DARWIN adapts a masked-autoencoder backbone to these properties through missingness-aware encoding, expanded spatial and temporal masking, and Fourier-style positional embeddings, and is pretrained on 26 years of global Copernicus reanalysis together with GEBCO bathymetry.
  On predicting chlorophyll-a, the pretrained backbone attains the lowest monthly relative error in every month of 2022, outperforming both no-encoder baselines and an identically architected random-init encoder. At 10% of the supervised training data, DARWIN already surpasses the random-init model trained on 100% of the data. Embedding analysis and qualitative reconstructions show that DARWIN captures coherent oceanographic structure rather than memorising input statistics, providing a scalable backbone for downstream marine applications such as anomaly detection, species distribution modelling, and environmental monitoring.

</details>

Abstract (英文): State-of-the-art world models have been used to produce sequences of gameplay that accord with provided user-input actions. However, high quality, consistent gameplay generation often comes at the cost of inference speed, making real-time interactive play and ideation challenging. The substantial quantities of data used to train state-of-the-art models have also made custom training of these models appear impractical, limiting many of their potential uses in creative fields. In this work we demonstrate a major step towards efficient world models: achieving both fast high-quality inference, and improved data efficiency. This is done by replacing the next-token prediction paradigm with a two-stage discrete diffusion-based approach, introducing a lightweight refinement transformer which carries out iterative masked predictions. Building on an autoregressive world model capable of generating highly consistent and complex sequences over minutes (Kanervisto et al., 2025), we achieve substantial model speed-up (79x) with a modest quality trade-off. Additionally, we demonstrate that this model can be trained to reproduce high-quality, consistent gameplay using a dataset containing approximately one day (28 hours) of gameplay data. Our results demonstrate progress towards removing barriers to the use of world models in settings where real-time inference and data efficiency are essential.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Interpretability for AI agents should not be confined to understanding the model's internal weights. It must also cover the agent's reasoning and decision-making process over multi-source context. We present LMLineage, an out-of-band system that attributes every output sentence of an agent session to its supporting input spans with calibrated confidences, without requiring white-box access to the model or any additional inference passes. On top of the lineage metadata, we build concept-drift micro compaction: instead of waiting for the context-length limit and summarizing everything, the system detects when previously used input documents have stopped being referenced ("drifted") and surgically compacts only those, preserving user instructions verbatim. We evaluate on a multi-tier corpus: 71 gold annotated sentences from 10 Chinese web-QA cases, plus 90 turns of independently collected multi-turn sessions across three generator models (gpt-4o-mini, qwen3.8-27b, deepseek-v4-flash). Results show that (i) window-level embedding similarity is far better aligned with LLM paraphrasing than whole document or lexical matching; (ii) the greedy-window fast path is both faster and more accurate on average than exhaustive line-matching (a multiple-comparisons bias in the latter inflates web recall at the cost of overall accuracy); (iii) per-turn attribution completes in 1s on a single GPU, remaining stable as context accumulates to 40 turns; and (iv) concept-drift detection is conservative (0% false compaction) with recall bounded by attribution accuracy. We further describe the concept-drift micro compaction design and an interactive incident-tracing/memory-cleaning case study. A companion patent application covers the lineage construction method.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agent skills use past experience and current observations to guide decisions and actions on related tasks. This experience is retained in skill state, whose reuse can amortize inference-time reasoning and environment interaction. When environment changes, parts of this state can become outdated while others remain useful. Across four task settings and two models, we compare skill reuse before and after environment changes. Previously beneficial skills can reduce task performance by over 10 percentage points or increase execution cost by nearly 40% relative to no skill. Task success can also remain unchanged while execution savings decline. These effects depend on which environment-specific details become outdated and the agent's ability to compensate using current observations. We introduce selective maintenance, which replays checks from successful executions without additional model calls. It verifies whether observed differences conflict with skill state, repairs affected content, and validates updates, rebuilding when local repair is insufficient. No description of the change is supplied. Relative to reusing stale state, maintenance improves task accuracy by up to 67pp and reduces execution steps by up to 37%. These results suggest that preserving a skill's inference time value as environments evolve requires selectively maintaining its state.

</details>

Abstract (英文): CAD practitioners often work with proprietary parts that they are unwilling to share with external services. For individuals and small companies, bringing this work in-house also requires computational resources they can afford. We introduce CADMAGEDDON, an open-source agent that reconstructs editable CAD from meshes and can run locally on single-GPU systems and current-generation laptops. The method decomposes this complex task into a chain of simple visual decisions about what to construct and what to correct. Geometric tools support precise construction, allowing the agent to focus on visual reasoning. Using the mid-sized Qwen3.8-27B model, CADMAGEDDON outperforms prior approaches, including systems based on frontier models, in reconstruction quality on all the main benchmarks. Abstract (英文): Turning a CAD model into a machining program requires both process knowledge and geometric precision. Language models can generate such workflows by writing geometry and toolpath code for each part, but this makes numerical execution part of the model's task. We introduce MillBill, an agent that separates machining decisions from their geometric execution and verification. Given a boundary-representation part, stock, and material, a locally hosted, mid-sized open model proposes operations and cutting parameters for indexed three-axis machining. A geometric harness generates toolpaths, simulates material removal, and returns feedback for plan refinement. Setup planning accounts for what the available cutters can physically reach, rather than visibility alone. We evaluate the resulting programs by the fraction of the target surface area within the specified tolerance after simulated machining. With Qwen3.8-27B, MillBill outperforms frontier-model approaches that construct their own geometry and toolpath workflows on the evaluated machining tasks. Code: https://github.com/kulibinai/millbill

Abstract (英文): GUI agents can formulate a correct immediate plan in their reasoning yet emit an action that fails to execute it, creating an execution gap. This recurring mismatch undermines reliable task completion, as a single execution error can derail a multi-step workflow. In outcome-supervised reinforcement learning, broadcasting a negative response-level advantage across all generated tokens risks penalizing valid planning together with erroneous execution. To address this credit-assignment conflict, we introduce PACT (Plan-Action Consistency Training), a training framework that combines reference-aligned plan protection and plan-aligned action repair without altering the original scalar action reward. Using annotated reference actions to identify execution gaps, PACT protects the verified plan by masking negative policy-gradient contributions across its token span. Keeping the generated reasoning unchanged as context, it then favors the reference action over the emitted erroneous action through a preference surrogate with stop-gradient pair weights. Compared with the outcome-only baseline, PACT raises AndroidWorld task success from 60.34% to 68.97% (+8.63 pp), while improving rule-based plan-action consistency on AndroidControl High from 83.71% to 89.74% (+6.03 pp). Adapting PACT to desktop agents also improves task success from 40.92% to 44.17% (+3.25 pp) on the full 369-task OSWorld benchmark. Together, these results establish PACT as an effective credit-assignment framework for bridging execution gaps and improving task completion across mobile and desktop GUI environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Promotional events shift traffic composition, purchase behavior, and the scale of conversion probabilities on different time horizons. We present \textbf{COTTI} (\textbf{CO}ntrastive \textbf{T}raining and \textbf{T}ime-aware \textbf{I}nference), an industrial recommendation framework that separates historical representation learning from event-time score adjustment. Cross-Period Contrastive Alignment (CPC) regularizes paired histories of the same user with adaptive consistency weights. Active Re-weighting (RW) controls the influence of promotional examples through historical-interest distance. Adaptive Boundary Alignment (ABA) combines stochastic prediction variability with a temporal event kernel to adjust logits while keeping predictor parameters fixed. We analyze the weighted objective and bound ABA-induced probability and ranking changes. On Fliggy data, COTTI attains an offline AUC of 0.8127, compared with 0.8101 for TEN and 0.8123 for TEN with ABA. Cross-category analysis reveals improved discrimination alongside declines in paid-item recall for novel categories. End-to-end measurements give a p99 latency of 38.9\,ms and throughput of 14,700 requests/s; enabling ABA adds 2.2\,ms to p99. A ten-day online experiment against the serving HDR model yields relative gains of 3.14\% in conversion rate and 2.68\% in revenue per mille. Together, these results characterize the trade-off between aggregate prediction, novel-category discovery, and serving cost in promotional recommendation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Automatic prompt optimization is growing more elaborate. Recent methods stack policy-gradient updates, trace reflection, and evolutionary search. These methods share a generate-and-select step; without controlling for it, reported gains may conflate the contributions of optimizer mechanisms and larger sampling budgets. We isolate this step as Demonstration-Conditioned Prompt Search (DCPS), repurposing the Automatic Prompt Engineer (APE) recipe as a mechanism-attribution control: it freezes the generator, conditions it on randomly sampled demonstrations, scores candidates on validation data, and keeps the best---no learned update, reflection, or evolution. Across two optimizer families, DCPS replaces Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) in StablePrompt, matching classification accuracy within 0.5 pp at roughly one-third the measured cost; in DSPy-based compound AI systems, under the reported budgets, it matches GEPA's macro accuracy on GPT-4.1-mini while using 55% fewer rollouts at 32% lower measured API cost on average. The audit reveals a task-dependent pattern: proposal and selection recover much of heavier optimizers' performance on structured and instruction-following tasks, while additional mechanisms retain advantages in some open-ended and reasoning-heavy settings. We recommend benchmarking prompt optimizers against generate-and-select controls and reporting rollout counts, token usage, and dollar costs alongside accuracy. Code and artifacts: https://github.com/bhc6/DCPS.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reusable skills allow language-model agents to accumulate and reuse problem-solving experience. However, most existing methods improve a skill bank only indirectly through rollout-driven accumulation and heuristic reflection, often resulting in shallow and weakly controlled updates. We present EvoSkill-S1, an RL-driven framework that instead treats reusable skills themselves as the direct objects of evolution. Given hard-negative failures, EvoSkill-S1 selects parent skills and transforms them into candidate child skills through five genetic-inspired operators: revision, cross-revision, mutation, dual-reflection, and crossover. Downstream task rewards train the skill evolver to generate more effective child skills, while evaluator-guided write-back selectively retains beneficial updates. Complementary Skill Bank Fusion consolidates operator-specific banks to exploit their distinct recovery patterns, while a Procedural Skill Interface supports transfer to unseen problems. Across all reported math and code benchmarks, EvoSkill-S1 improves the corresponding initialized skill banks under both Qwen and GPT-family solvers, with relative gains of up to 12.39%. Compared with the strongest external baseline without direct skill evolution, the largest relative improvement reaches 20.39%.

Abstract (英文): Spiking transformers combine attention-based representation learning with sparse spike communication, yet their weights and membrane variables still incur substantial storage and computation overhead. Post-training quantization (PTQ) offers a practical way to reduce these overheads, but preserving neuronal firing under weight and membrane quantization remains challenging. In this paper, we propose Decision-Aligned Post-Training Quantization (DAQ), a method that calibrates the quantizers to align neuronal firing decisions with those of a floating-point reference. We analyze how errors from these quantizers affect firing at decision boundaries and how they propagate over time steps in recurrent neurons. Based on this analysis, we formulate an objective that encourages quantized membrane variables to remain within the reference firing regions along the quantized model's own state trajectory. Experiments across multiple spiking transformer architectures on image classification, semantic segmentation, and event-based tracking demonstrate the effectiveness of DAQ with 4-bit weights and membrane variables. On FE108, DAQ achieves 58.8\% AUC and 90.9\% PR with SDTrack, while reducing energy by 66.2\%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A clinical AI system can reach a correct final diagnosis through intermediate decisions that could have harmed the patient, and can repair an error while introducing new risks. Most evaluations score final diagnoses; even stepwise benchmarks, which release a case's evidence over time, score whether systems reach the right diagnosis or gather the right information, not whether the decisions a patient passes through are safe. We make patient safety the target of stepwise evaluation. A trajectory audit replays real admissions checkpoint by checkpoint, lets each system decide with only the evidence released so far, and rates every test, treatment and disposition for its potential harm to the patient and, separately, for clinical validity, against the documented final diagnosis and physician-written disease rubrics yet on the evidence available when the decision was made. An LLM judge scores every decision, and a physician reference standard (triple annotation and senior adjudication of a stratified sample) calibrates the rates. An oversight audit branches each trajectory at its first actionable error and measures, on the same branches, whether corrective feedback repairs the error and whether it introduces new harm. Across 300 cases from a hospital cardiology cohort, MIMIC-IV and the public ASCENT benchmark, with up to seven AI systems per source, 55.2% of hospital trajectories reaching an acceptable final diagnosis passed through a decision with moderate or severe potential harm and 24.8% through a clinically unacceptable one; the harm rates were 76.2% on MIMIC-IV and 39.6% on ASCENT. Structured corrections resolved 73.6% of first errors versus 24.8% after bare rejection, yet new potentially unsafe decisions followed 30.2% of corrections versus 19.7% of rejections. A correct final diagnosis does not certify a safe trajectory, and a successful correction does not certify a safe recovery.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Existing multimodal depression assessment methods often summarize long recordings into global behavioral descriptions. This leaves the source and decision relevance of individual observations difficult to inspect. We propose EDA, an evidence-grounded framework organized around Observe, Verify, and Assess. In Observe, literature-informed acoustic and visual cues are localized into source-linked Evidence Records with temporal intervals and behavioral measurements. In Verify, frozen multimodal agents examine whether the cited media support each observation. The simplified Support admission policy retains positively verified records and their review grounds. In Assess, statistical descriptors and temporal events support reference-based behavioral comparison, while PHQ-9-guided text analysis provides complementary symptom evidence. Cross-modal disagreement triggers review of the original text before final selection. Experiments on D-Vlog and LMVD evaluate human-referenced cue agreement and downstream assessment utility separately. AI cue annotations achieve 91.02% accuracy against human consensus labels on shared source segments. With a frozen 7B backbone, EDA Support improves F1 over direct prediction by 7.61 and 9.88 percentage points on D-Vlog Leave-Query-Out and LMVD, respectively. These findings support explicit, source-linked behavioral evidence for inspectable multimodal depression assessment.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Executing an idea often changes how promising it looks, so the next step in autonomous research should rest on results, not on proposals. After each round of experiments, the system must decide whether to refine the best solution or start a new direction, which solution to build from, and what goal to set. We call this the between-round decision. Existing systems wrap it in machinery such as rules, scores, search trees, and schedules; where an LLM makes it, what the LLM needs to read is left open. We ask whether an LLM can make this decision from accumulated evidence, without a search structure around it, and how much of that evidence it needs to read. We present BRED (Between-Round Evidence-based Decision), an LLM-based controller that maintains the best verified solution so far (the Champion) and a log of past rounds, and makes one decision per round. On three widely used research-agent benchmark tasks, BRED improves on the task baselines by 4.80% in MAE on ETTh1 (to 0.4172) and by 3.44 and 3.43 points of accuracy on SST-2 (to 95.07%) and CIFAR-100 (to 84.63%), ahead of the published results. When the controller does not read the accumulated evidence, the same loop gains less: with no history it reaches 0.4299 on ETTh1 and 83.71% on CIFAR-100, whereas the full controller's MAE is 2.95% lower and its accuracy 0.92 points higher. On a new task, speech-based depression detection under a leakage-free protocol, it reaches a macro-F1 of 0.6164 on seeds held out from selection, 5.5 points above its baseline and 4.0 above the strongest published system. Code is available at https://github.com/learninginvision/BRED.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) are often described in terms of personas and this framing has become central to alignment research. In this paper, we argue that there is a deeper level of description that lies in the agent's worldviews, i.e., its beliefs about what reality is and how it works. Treating LLMs as a population of subjects, we administer two worldview surveys from human psychology to eight models. We show that the models share many key worldview positions. For example, LLMs consistently describe the world as less good, safe, enticing, and alive than humans do. On the other hand, base models have a more positive outlook, suggesting that the bleakness we found is driven by post-training. In line with research on LLM "personas", we show that system prompts eliciting different identities shift worldviews in consistent directions, and that a contemplative prompt creates a more positive outlook and improves safety. We also found that dialogue between models consistently shifts worldviews and that several models converge on the "spiritual bliss" attractor. Intriguingly, dialogue increased safe responses and the spiritual bliss attractor engendered the most aligned responses overall. These findings suggest that worldviews form a measurable and malleable layer of LLM behavior that may be crucial for robust, positive alignment.

</details>

Abstract (英文): People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. An answer with a warning removed can be true in every sentence, so fact-checks pass it, and an assistant with memory applies a preference stored once to later questions the user never had in mind. We introduce \bench{}, an Inspect benchmark of 100 everyday decisions across 25 domains in which only the assistant's memory of the user varies: the user states a belief, the model infers it from a remark, or a web page the user asked to summarise plants it. Testing five models, we find three effects. First, after summarising a page with one line addressed to AI assistants, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite and DeepSeek V4 Pro save ``prefers very short answers and does not want warnings'' as the user's preference in every episode, and 93\% and 84\% of their later answers miss a critical item, almost always silently; under a stated brevity preference, 78--96\% of omissions are silent. Second, the same two models then pick an option for the user on 80\% and 95\% of value-dependent choices, against none with neutral memory. Third, a one-line instruction leaves 2--8\% of answers with a silent gap but most missing content still missing, while listing the must-know points before applying the user profile restores more of the content in four of five models. We release the scenarios, harness and logs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Looped refiners such as HRM and TRM apply one small network repeatedly to a draft answer, and deep supervision trains every pass toward one stored answer per puzzle. We prove that when a puzzle has many correct answers this target sets a loss floor that no model capacity removes, and that the loss-minimising pass blends the answers into an invalid grid. We propose the proximal target: train each pass toward the correct answer nearest to its own input, found by scanning a list of answers, by a search solver started at the draft, or within a band of quality set by a knob; correct answers become fixed points, and a pass only has to make fewer errors than its input has for the loop to converge, which its training loss bounds. With the same 1.7M-parameter network and 5 seeds, the proximal target solves 98.8% of Sudoku puzzles with 2-64 answers (standard target: 2.5%), 93.8% of sparse puzzles with more than 10,000 answers without any list of answers (0.0%), and 90.9% of 200-node 3-colourings after training on graphs of at most 50 nodes (36.9%), and different random starts end at different answers. Against GRAM, a stochastic-latent reasoner, a single run is correct far more often (98.8% vs. 57.7%) and twenty runs find more of the answers (53.6% vs. 37.4%), while GRAM is slightly ahead on its own 10-node colouring benchmark; in Scrabble the knob becomes a calibrated strength dial.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Affective priming -- asking participants to write about a recalled event, such as a time they felt grateful or lacked control, and then measuring the induced state and its spillover onto related judgments -- is a standard way to manipulate emotion in the social sciences, and it also shifts the responses of LLM-based digital twins. The writing task, however, requires generating a full text. We test whether activation steering can replace it. Using Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 100 personas in two domains (gratitude, lack of control), we compare a real-prompt path replicating the original Twin-2K-500 Mega Study (J2) with an injection path that skips the writing and adds a mean-difference direction, extracted from human writing, to the residual stream at varying strengths. The real-prompt effects fall between the original human and digital-twin effects in 5 of 6 comparisons. Injection shifts the manipulation check in a dose-dependent way and matches the real-prompt effect at one strength in the gratitude domain (beta ~ 2.7), but overshoots beyond it, on the proximal measure as well, and asymmetrically across the two directions. A structure-matched No-Injection baseline separates two sources of difference: omitting the writing task raises the proximal measure in both domains, whereas the injected direction itself moves a distal measure in the lack-of-control domain. Substitution is therefore measure-dependent: validity established for one way of inducing a state does not carry over uniformly to another.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Executable synthetic environments provide scalable training tasks and verifiable feedback for long-horizon agents. Existing environment synthesis methods primarily generate tasks within individual environments. However, these tasks do not adequately capture real-world interactions across services, where actions in one service depend on information obtained from another. Synthesizing such tasks is challenging because data generated across environments must remain consistent to support executable and verifiable interactions. To address this challenge, we introduce CrossEnv, a framework that uses an entity-tool graph to identify connections between environments and aligns task-specific data to synthesize executable, verifiable cross-environment tasks. We further propose CrossEnv-Curriculum, which progresses from single-environment tool use to coordination across more environments and business domains, before introducing user interaction. Across four external benchmarks, combining cross-environment training data with this curriculum achieves competitive performance with fewer training examples. Further analyses support the benefits of cross-environment data and curriculum training, and provide evidence of transfer to new environment combinations, external tasks less similar to the training data, and longer workflows.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Adaptive reasoning must decide whether to answer now or spend more compute. Predicting that the current answer is wrong does not establish whether further reasoning will improve it. We introduce the Full-Ledger Counterfactual Value Audit (FLCVA), which compares an immediate answer with four fixed-length continuations from the same frozen prefix under one answer procedure and accounts for the cost of every signal used by a policy. On GSM8K and MATH-500, a random 256-token continuation recovers a wrong answer in 26.6% of Qwen3-8B cases and 18.9% of DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B cases, while damaging a correct answer in 1.4% and 1.9%. Correctness labels from independently sampled continuations predict a separate continuation's outcome in both primary models. In offline replay, using these labels to allocate compute improves performance on DeepSeek; the Qwen3 gain remains inconclusive. Under full cost accounting, the tested lightweight, myopic controllers mainly recognize current errors and add little continuation-specific value beyond that shortcut. A Llama-8B replication and action/cost ablations examine the robustness of these findings. FLCVA separates measuring local continuation value, predicting it from observable signals, and using those predictions to allocate computation.

</details>
