### [368] [Hyperspectral remote sensing of cover crop traits for decision support in precision nitrogen management](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rse.2026.115759)
*Jyoti S. Jennewein,Wells Dean Hively,Brian T. Lamb,S. Chris Reberg-Horton,Steven Brian Mirsky*

与遥感交叉或改进方案: Remote sensing cross-disciplinary scheme unavailable

Abstract (英文): Spaceborne hyperspectral sensors offer opportunities to quantify crop traits and inform variable rate nitrogen management, yet their operational use remains limited. We evaluated PRISMA and EMIT imagery across four winter cover crop seasons in Maryland, U.S., to predict traits required by the open source Cover Crop Nitrogen Calculator (CC-NCALC) decision support tool. Traits included aboveground biomass, nitrogen concentration (N%), non-structural carbohydrates (NSC%), holocellulose (HCL%), and lignin (LIG%). Using these traits as inputs, the CC-NCALC estimates nitrogen credits from cover crops based on decomposition dynamics informed by trait data, soil, and weather conditions. Nitrogen credits are the estimated amount of plant available nitrogen that a cover crop contributes to the subsequently planted cash crop. Trait models were developed using partial least squares regression with several cross validation strategies. Biomass ( R 2 = 0.70–0.87; RMSE = 661–951 kg ha −1 ), NSC% ( R 2 = 0.69–0.72; RMSE = 4.53–7.63%), and HCL% ( R 2 = 0.65–0.70; RMSE = 5.04–7.50%) predictions were strong. But HCL% and NSC% predictions degraded under cross-scene and cross-species validation ( R 2 = 0.47–0.54; RMSE = 6.06–9.18%). N% ( R 2 = 0.15–0.79; RMSE = 0.46–0.74%) and LIG% ( R 2 = 0.02–0.35; RMSE = 1.22–1.83%) predictions were more variable; predictions using EMIT were more accurate than predictions using PRISMA, but both approaches demonstrated the need for further increases in accuracies of retrieval approaches for these two traits. Spring trait maps from April 2024 were ingested into the CC-NCALC to estimate nitrogen credits, which ranged from −14.58 to 45.65 kg N ha −1 , with higher values associated with greater legume biomass. Inter-scene comparisons demonstrate EMIT's increased spectral fidelity and trait prediction accuracy compared to PRISMA. Results demonstrate that integrating spaceborne hyperspectral imagery with decision support systems can generate site-specific nitrogen credit maps, advancing hyperspectral remote sensing toward operational nutrient management. Future missions will be critical to help scale these approaches for routine on-farm use.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The melting of excess ground ice in ice-rich permafrost regions causes elevated ground subsidence through soil thaw consolidation at landscape scales. The increased subsidence due to melting of excess ice is unaccounted for in current InSAR-based retrievals of season thaw depth, or active layer thickness (ALT); this omission may lead to overapproximated ALT estimates compared to in situ. We processed Sentinel-1 interferometric data spanning 2018–2023 over the Drew Point and Teshekpuk Lake region. Using a novel degree-day of thaw regression on InSAR displacement time series, we estimated total seasonal subsidence and found elevated subsidence during warmer years which we attribute to the melting of excess ice. Integrating these seasonal subsidence estimates into the remotely sensed active layer thickness (ReSALT) algorithm yielded consistently overapproximated ALT estimates. In conjunction with independent estimates of ALT, we exploited this positive bias in ReSALT to estimate excess ice within the active layer. We found agreement between InSAR estimates of excess ice and in situ measurements within uncertainty for warmer years. We observed a positive spatial correlation between seasonal subsidence and excess ice in the active layer with proximity to surface hydrological features. Furthermore, we observed that InSAR-derived subsidence decreases with older drained lakes, and InSAR-derived excess ice decreases with increasingly degraded ice wedge polygon type. We discovered that thaw season intensity has a first order effect on the temporal variability of seasonal subsidence and excess ice content, and the success of our method is contingent upon warm thaw seasons.

</details>

### [371] [Tracking US tidal marsh extent and change with dense Landsat time series and comparisons with existing products](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rse.2026.115779)
*Xiucheng Yang,Sara Knox,Shi Qiu,Kevin D. Kroeger,Zhiliang Zhu,Scott Covington,Zhe Zhu*

与遥感交叉或改进方案: Remote sensing cross-disciplinary scheme unavailable

Abstract (英文): Tidal marsh changes encompass both categorical cover change (i.e., extent change) and condition change, such as alterations in vegetation composition and hydrological regime, which together regulate ecosystem resilience and carbon cycling. Yet, most existing products rely on selective image compositing under similar tidal conditions or statistical values over fixed intervals, followed by epoch-based comparisons. These approaches are mainly designed to capture differences in marsh extent, while condition change within the same tidal marsh cover type remains largely unresolved despite its ecological importance. Here, we present a tide-informed dense time-series framework, DEtection and Characterization of cOastal tiDal wEtlands change (DECODE v2), for continuous monitoring of tidal marsh extent and change (including condition change and cover change, such as tidal marsh gain or loss) across the conterminous United States from 1988 to 2023. Two key methodological advances underpin this framework and enable global transferability: integration of a global empirical ocean tide model to estimate water levels for each Landsat observation, and an adaptive temporal normalization strategy to ensure consistent observation density across single- and dual-satellite eras. Stratified random sampling confirms strong performance in both tidal marsh extent and change mapping, supporting a shift from traditional binary extent mapping and extent-only change detection to a continuous monitoring strategy. Cross-product comparisons show that DECODE improves spatial consistency and reduces disagreement with reference interpretation for tidal marsh extent and cover change, while also providing additional information that is largely absent from existing products, including separation of cover change and condition change, change timing, and repeated change events at the same location. Critically, condition change dominated cumulative tidal marsh change across the conterminous United States (CONUS), accounting for approximately 81.1 ± 2.0% of the cumulative annual change area and greatly exceeding cumulative cover change (13,058 ± 1366 km 2 versus 3038 ± 216 km 2 ). By characterizing not only where tidal marsh extent changes occur, but also what type of change occurs, when it occurs, and how often it recurs, DECODE provides a more comprehensive basis for assessing ecosystem resilience, restoration effectiveness, and blue carbon dynamics.

</details>
